IMF Country Report No. 26/163

## KYRGYZ REPUBLIC

July 2026

## 2026 ARTICLE IV CONSULTATION—PRESS RELEASE; STAFF REPORT; AND STATEMENT BY THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR FOR KYRGYZ REPUBLIC

Under Article IV of the IMF's Articles of Agreement, the IMF holds bilateral discussions with members, usually every year. In the context of the 2026 Article IV consultation with the Kyrgyz Republic, the following documents have been released and are included in this package:

\- A Press Release summarizing the views of the Executive Board as expressed during its June 3, 2026, consideration of the staff report that concluded the Article IV consultation with the Kyrgyz Republic.

\- The Staff Report prepared by a staff team of the IMF for the Executive Board's consideration on June 3, 2026, following discussions that ended on April 1, 2026, with the officials of the Kyrgyz Republic on economic developments and policies. Based on information available at the time of these discussions, the staff report was completed on May 14, 2026.

• An Informational Annex prepared by the IMF staff.

\- A Debt Sustainability Analysis prepared by the staffs of the IMF the International Development Association.

• A Statement by the Executive Director for the Kyrgyz Republic.

The documents listed below have been or will be separately released.

Selected Issues

The IMF's transparency policy allows for the deletion of market-sensitive information and premature disclosure of the authorities' policy intentions in published staff reports and other documents.

Copies of this report are available to the public from

## IMF Executive Board Concludes 2026 Article IV Consultation with Kyrgyz Republic

## FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE

\- The Kyrgyz Republic has sustained strong economic growth for the fourth consecutive year, but inflation is now above the central bank's target.

• Growth is expected to moderate as reexport and trade-related activities plateau.

\- The favorable economic performance provides a window of opportunity to enhance resilience, strengthen policy buffers, and support inclusive growth.

Washington, DC – June 4, 2026: The Executive Board of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) completed the Article IV Consultation for the Kyrgyz Republic. $^{1}$ The authorities need more time to consider the publication of the Staff Report prepared for this consultation. $^{2}$

The Kyrgyz Republic continues to record strong economic performance as the economy transitions from low-income status. Since 2022, expanded trade flows, robust remittance and capital inflows, and strong construction activity supported by government spending have underpinned rapid growth. Output is estimated to have grown by 11 percent in 2025. At the same time, inflation rose to 11 percent in March 2026, well above the central bank's 5–7 percent target range.

Growth is expected to moderate as recent trade-related gains fade but will be buoyed by large infrastructure projects over the medium term. Inflation is likely to remain high through 2027 before easing gradually if macroeconomic policies are tightened appropriately. After fiscal surpluses in 2023–25, the budget is projected to move into deficit in 2026, mainly because of higher public wages and capital spending. Public debt remains sustainable, but financing needs are sizable. Strong remittances and high gold prices are expected to support the external position.

Even though the Kyrgyz Republic has limited exposure to the war in the Middle East, sustained high oil prices could add to inflation pressures. With heightened global uncertainty, accelerating reforms will be key to enhance resilience, strengthen policy buffers, and support inclusive growth.

## Executive Board Assessment

Executive Directors agreed with the thrust of the staff appraisal. They welcomed the Kyrgyz Republic's rapid economic growth and declining poverty, while noting downside risks, emerging signs of overheating, and elevated inflation pressures. In this context, Directors underscored the need for careful policy recalibration, supported by Fund capacity development, to enhance resilience, strengthen buffers, and foster private sector-led inclusive growth.

Directors underscored the need to bolster the fiscal position by strengthening revenue mobilization to create space for priority infrastructure and targeted social spending, while containing expenditure on energy subsidies and wages. They called for enhancing public financial, investment, and debt management to strengthen efficiency, resilience, and fiscal sustainability. Directors also emphasized the need to fully capture quasi-fiscal operations and SOE risks, including ensuring that the operations of the Stabilization Fund are transparently reflected in fiscal reporting and risk assessments.

Directors supported maintaining an appropriately tight and data-dependent monetary policy stance until inflation durably returns to the central bank's target range. They emphasized the importance of strengthening the operational framework and encouraged the authorities to strengthen the independence of the central bank. Greater exchange rate flexibility and more diversified reserves would further enhance external resilience.

Noting rapid credit growth, high NPLs and a deepening bank–sovereign nexus, Directors called for heightened financial supervisory vigilance and capacity, focusing on a risk-based approach and proactive macroprudential measures. They also noted the need for enhanced AML/CFT measures and stronger supervision and governance of virtual asset service providers.

Directors called for ambitious structural reforms to support private sector-led growth. They agreed that priority areas include SOE reform and strengthening the business environment, competition, the rule of law, and anti-corruption. Directors supported ongoing efforts to reduce informality and labor market rigidities, invest in human capital and digital infrastructure, and harness the gains of AI. Strengthening external sector and national account statistics is essential to enhance macroeconomic surveillance.

# KYRGYZ REPUBLIC

## STAFF REPORT FOR THE 2026 ARTICLE IV CONSULTATION

May 14, 2026

## KEY ISSUES

Context: The Kyrgyz Republic has sustained strong economic growth for the fourth consecutive year. The outlook, is however clouded by significant downside risks. The overall favorable economic performance provides a window of opportunity to accelerate reforms that enhance resilience, strengthen policy buffers, and make room for private sector-led growth.

## Recommendations:

\- Fiscal policy. Investment in infrastructure and human capital needs to be balanced with the creation of fiscal space and prudent debt management. In this context, stronger PFM measures and proper budget classification would enhance the efficiency and transparency of public spending. In the longer term, fiscal prospects would be safeguarded by a careful assessment of the public pension system.

\- Monetary and exchange rate policies. To bring inflation in line with the National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic's target, the monetary policy stance may need to be tightened further should inflation not be on a firmly downward trajectory, in the context of reinforcing the independence of the central bank. The economy's resilience to external shocks would be enhanced by greater exchange rate flexibility and diversification of reserve composition.

\- Financial sector policies. The financial sector would benefit from strengthened supervisory capacity and risk monitoring.

\- Structural reforms—in particular, creating the space for private sector-led growth—remain crucial, in line with past staff advice.

\- Data adequacy. Strengthening the quality of data, especially in the external sector and in national accounts statistics, is essential.

Discussions were held during March 18–April 1, 2026 in Bishkek. The staff team comprised Dmitry Gershenson (head), Khaled Abdelkader, Nasir Rao, Jean van Houtte (all MCD), Anh Nguyen (FAD), Maksym Markevych (LEG), Farid Talishli (Resident Representative), Erkeaim Shambetova and Anvar Muratkhanov, with support from Aigerim Toigonbaeva (all Resident Representative office). Patryk Loszewski and Lilia Kadyrberdieva (OED) participated in key meetings. Nihal Haider, Elyad Shojaei, and Svetlana Zolotareva assisted from the headquarters. The mission met with central bank Governor Baketaev, Minister of Finance Suinaliev, former central bank Governor Turgunbaev, and other officials, as well as representatives of the business and diplomatic communities and development partners.

## CONTEXT

1. The Kyrgyz Republic is transitioning from low-income status. Since 2022, expanded trade flows, robust remittance and capital inflows, and strong construction activity supported by government spending have underpinned rapid growth. Real GDP expanded by 47 percent between 2021 and 2025, making the Kyrgyz Republic the third-fastest-growing economy in the world. During this period, per capita GDP in U.S. dollars doubled, reaching 3,100 in 2025.

2. The current favorable economic environment provides an opportunity to accelerate reforms. In June 2025, the authorities adopted the National Development Program (NDP) 2030. The NDP lays out the national objectives of (i) raising nominal GDP from the current US\$23 billion to at least US\$30 billion and reaching GDP per capita of US\$4,500; (ii) achieving annual GDP growth of 8 percent; and (iii) capping the unemployment rate at 5 percent. The authorities broadly agree with staff on the reform priorities to achieve these goals but prefer a longer horizon for their implementation (See Annex I).

## RECENT MACROECONOMIC DEVELOPMENTS

## 3. Signs of overheating are becoming

evident. Output is estimated to have expanded by 11 percent in 2025, owing to strong performance of the construction and trade-related sectors and sustained government spending. Reflecting the growing real wages and ample credit to the private sector, inflation has risen from 6 percent at end-2024 to 11 percent in March 2026, well above the central bank's target range (5-7 percent).

![](images/5be45164fd274c7bc878941a68b2215933bd9833f53edc5f4b03d6cb28ce5ec3.jpg)

## 4. Net financing needs were large despite the fiscal surplus. The overall fiscal

surplus increased from 2.0 percent of GDP in 2024 to 2.6 percent of GDP in 2025. This reflects large non-tax revenues (10.3 percent of GDP), including proceeds from the liquidation of seized assets and profit transfer from the central bank. $^{1}$ Despite the surplus, the net financing needs were large (estimated at 8.5 percent of GDP in 2025) due to the acquisition of financial assets, mostly in the form of capital injections into SOEs, such as the State Mortgage Company, energy companies and financial institutions, including Eldik Bank.

5. The external environment remained favorable in 2025, but significant errors and omissions in the balance of payments continue to complicate the analysis. As was the case in recent years, errors and omissions remain significant (17 percent of GDP in the first three quarters of 2025), likely reflecting unrecorded trade and financial flows. $^{2}$ The current account deficit, adjusted for these unrecorded flows, is estimated to have increased from 6.4 percent of GDP in 2024 to around 7.5 percent of GDP in 2025, driven by higher imports and lower gold exports. At the same time, international reserves rose—from 4 to 6 months of imports—owing mainly to the revaluation of monetary gold holdings and to the partial conversion of the stock of non-monetary gold into monetary gold. $^{3}$

## 6. The country's first eurobond issuance established the country's standing in

international markets, at a cost. The proceeds (US\$0.7 billion or 3.1 percent of GDP) were used to recapitalize the state-owned Eldik Bank, which then lent most of the money to the Government at a favorable interest rate. The resulting funds were subsequently deposited at Eldik Bank with a view to financing energy and housing projects. $^{4}$ As a result, the downward public debt trajectory—observed since 2021—reversed, with the stock of public debt rising from 36 percent of GDP at end-2024 to 39 percent at end-2025.

7. The financial sector is small but growing fast. It is dominated by commercial banks (both state-owned and private) and virtual asset service providers (VASPs). The commercial banks are largely profitable, well capitalized, and stable, notwithstanding the sanctioning of four small banks. $^{5}$ In 2025, VASPs increased their turnover threefold—exceeding GDP—while AML/CFT supervisory resources and capacity remain limited. Moreover, the Ministry of Finance issued a first-of-its-kind stablecoin (USDKG)—in the amount of US\$50 million (0.2 percent of GDP)—to facilitate cross-border transactions. $^{6}$

## OUTLOOK AND RISKS

8. Growth is projected to decline from 11.1 percent in 2025 to 6.1 percent in 2026, reflecting the unwinding of temporary drivers that boosted activity in recent years. Re-export trade-related activities—which contributed significantly to growth since 2022—are expected to plateau, while domestic demand is expected to moderate assuming tighter macroeconomic policies aimed at containing overheating. Large infrastructure projects will continue to support activity but are not expected to fully offset the normalization of trade-related flows $^{7}$ . Starting this year, growth is projected to average around 6 percent before gradually converging to its estimated potential rate of about 5.3 percent. The fiscal deficit is projected at 4 percent of GDP in 2026, decreasing to 3 percent of GDP over the medium term, with public debt rising to 41 percent of GDP by 2031. The Debt Sustainability Analysis (DSA) indicates that public debt remains sustainable with moderate risks of external and overall debt distress (see DSA). The overall balance of payments is expected to remain in deficit, leading to a gradual reduction of the country's international reserves, with import coverage declining from 6 months in 2025 to 3 months in 2031 under current policies.

9. Inflation would be expected to return to within the central bank's target range only in 2030. With GDP growth already above potential, the planned large expenditures, and the monetary policy stance, inflation is projected to remain in double digits in 2026 and 2027 before gradually declining to under 7 percent in 2030.

10. Risks are tilted to the downside (Annex I). External risks stem from possible trade reversals, which could undermine the high growth momentum, and a fall in gold prices, which could negatively affect fiscal revenues, exports, and the value of reserves. Domestic risks include entrenched inflation and fiscal vulnerabilities. Financial integrity risks and international sanctions related to third countries could expose banks to regulatory and enforcement actions, straining correspondent relationships, increasing AMF/CFT compliance costs, and negatively impacting cross-border financial flows and trade. In the absence of a strong legal, supervisory and regulatory framework, the fast growth of virtual assets could undermine the conduct of monetary policy and the stability of the banking sector.

11. Under a severe global shock scenario, the Kyrgyz Republic would be affected directly by higher global oil prices and indirectly through trade and financial flows. While fuel supply is largely secured through existing contracts with Russia, higher global oil prices would still pass through to domestic costs and add to the already high inflation. Given that part of recent growth has been supported by re-export trade, an economic slowdown in regional trading partners could soften these trade flows and weigh on activity. Similarly, remittances and, hence, the current account balance, could come under pressure if economic conditions in labor-importing economies weaken. Overall, even though these channels could add to macroeconomic pressures, the impact is expected to be manageable.

## Authorities' Views

12. The authorities envisage that the strong growth of the last few years will continue. With the state as the engine of economic development, growth would be supported by large investment projects. The authorities are mindful of the risks posed by inflation but consider that the current monetary policy stance—together with well-anchored inflation expectations—will help maintain medium-term price stability within the 5–7 percent range and ensure macroeconomic resilience. The authorities view the war in the Middle East as a risk factor for the economy, especially in its impact on energy prices and inflation, but not as fundamentally altering the baseline at this stage.

## POLICY DISCUSSIONS

## A. Fiscal Policy

13. Creating fiscal space is key to supporting spending on infrastructure and human capital. Staff estimates that tax revenue could be raised by up to 4 percent of GDP through streamlining VAT exemptions and special tax regimes, increasing the progressivity of the personal income tax, and strengthening revenue administration. $^{8}$ Channeling a larger share of the profits of Kumtor (a state-owned gold producer) to the budget would provide additional revenues. $^{9}$ This is particularly important as expenditures (and the fiscal deficit) are projected to increase in the coming years, reflecting investment in physical and human capital and the large (2 percent of GDP) salary increase for public sector workers envisaged in 2026. In addition to its beneficial impact on debt dynamics, a gradual recalibration of fiscal spending toward a less pro-cyclical stance would help moderate demand pressures.

14. Prudent debt management is needed to preserve fiscal sustainability. Flagship infrastructure projects are being financed by concessional lending commitments from IFIs. The use of the Eurobond proceeds to recapitalize Eldik Bank, however, generated fiscal costs and entailed sustainability risks, particularly if future returns do not offset the additional debt-service obligations. Moreover, external borrowing without gradual amortization, such as the contracted eurobond, heighten rollover risks. $^{10}$

15. Stronger PFM measures and proper budget classification are needed to enhance the efficiency and transparency of public spending. A Fiscal Transparency Evaluation would help identify gaps in fiscal reporting, forecasting, budgeting, and fiscal risk management. Reform priorities include improving the targeting of social spending, operationalizing the SOE fiscal risk framework, and either strengthening the governance of the Stabilization Fund (SF) or dissolving it. $^{11}$ To better assess the underlying fiscal position, proper classification of various financing operations is essential. $^{12}$

16. Careful assessment of the public pension system would safeguard long-term fiscal prospects. While recent salary increases improve the current balance of the pay-as-you-go scheme, they imply a substantial increase in benefit payments when today's active civil servants retire and as life expectancy increases. A basic modeling exercise of public pensions shows that the pension burden (ratio of benefit payments to contributions) is slated to increase over time. The authorities need to quantify the long-term fiscal trajectory of the public pension system and its burden on the national budget (see Annex X: Assessing Fiscal Risk in the Public Pension of the Kyrgyz Republic).

## Authorities' Views

17. The authorities broadly agree with staff's assessment and reaffirm their commitment to fiscal and debt sustainability. They express confidence in maintaining a disciplined fiscal stance, supported by ongoing tax administration reforms and robust growth. The authorities do not envisage introducing a progressive personal income tax system in place of the current flat tax regime over the medium term. Non-tax revenues this year are expected to remain at last year's level. They view the SF as an extrabudgetary fund established to support the socioeconomic development of the Kyrgyz Republic. The authorities emphasize that external borrowing would be primarily associated with high-return infrastructure investments.

## B. Monetary and Exchange Rate Policies

18. The National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic (NBKR) may need to tighten its monetary policy stance should inflation not be firmly on a declining trajectory. In 2025, the central bank raised the policy rate from 9 to 11 percent, and again to 12 percent in February 2026. The overnight deposit rate—which allows the central bank to directly remunerate banks for their liquidity—was raised as well (from 4 to 5 percent), but is too low to be fully effective, as financial conditions remain accommodative (for further discussion, see the forthcoming SIP “Beyond the Policy Rate: A Financial Conditions Perspective”). Moreover, unsterilized purchases of gold and the large transfer of central bank profits to the Treasury have added to inflation pressures. The NBKR should signal its intent to keep real interest rates positive and contain excessive credit growth. It should also take steps to enhance monetary policy transmission, including by (i) curtailing gold purchases, (ii) establishing a symmetric and narrower interest rate corridor, and (iii) using longer-maturity instruments to mop up excess liquidity. In this regard, raising the overnight deposit rate would be an important first step.

19. Strengthening the independence and governance of the NBKR remains critical to safeguard price stability. Repeated transfers of central bank profits to the budget while its capital remains below statutory thresholds risk undermining institutional credibility and the conduct of monetary policy. The authorities would be encouraged to uphold the provisions of the constitutional law governing the NBKR, discontinue regular profit transfers until capital is adequately restored, and ensure that monetary policy decisions remain fully aligned with the price stability mandate.

20. Greater exchange rate flexibility and diversification of reserve composition would enhance resilience to shocks. While international reserves have increased due to the revaluation of gold holdings, the high concentration in gold exposes the economy to commodity-price volatility.

Continued diversification of reserves and conversion of eligible gold into monetary gold would strengthen external resilience and further enhance reserve management.

21. The External Sector Assessment points to an overvaluation of the Som. It indicates that the external position of the Kyrgyz Republic in 2025 remained substantially weaker than the level implied by medium-term fundamentals and desirable policies (Annex II). Reasons include the strong domestic demand and a high import content of consumption, leading to a gradual decrease in reserves over the medium term. A more flexible exchange rate, reforms aimed at improving competitiveness, and a countercyclical fiscal stance to contain demand pressures would support the external position of the country. Restrictions on export of US dollar cash introduced in 2023 constitute an outflow CFM under the Institutional View on the Liberalization and Management of Capital Flows. $^{13}$

![](images/9ba27db68ddf9cbe10ecbbb2c7edf8f478685e358e8a44658a2701d6fda62049.jpg)

![](images/b75f7ea3a6771c1c00cca03fa00007b0f9ffcf1976ad785a6cae7bf347df5bfa.jpg)

## Authorities' Views

22. The authorities reaffirmed their commitment to price stability. They consider inflation expectations to be well anchored and stand ready to implement the necessary monetary policy measures to bring inflation back to the target range of 5-7 percent within the medium-term.

## C. Financial Sector Policies

23. Supervisory vigilance is warranted in view of elevated NPLs, the rapid expansion of credit, and directed lending. At over 10 percent of total loans, NPLs remain elevated, which suggests pockets of vulnerability. Even though the credit gap is still negative, its upward trajectory implies heightened risks, especially since the surge in credit to the economy in 2025 was led by

consumer lending. $^{14}$ The authorities would be encouraged to implement (i) macroprudential regulatory measures (such as countercyclical capital buffers, limits on loan-to-value ratios, and liquidity requirements to ensure resilience to shocks); and (ii) strengthened supervisory review of credit risk management practices.

24. Directed lending by state-owned commercial banks, including for energy projects, should be discouraged. Such lending could exacerbate uncertainty around the assessments of the projects' underlying economic and financial viability, since the lending is not necessarily guided by commercial principles. The use of Eurobond proceeds to recapitalize Eldik Bank may have added unnecessary complexity to the balance sheet of the bank. Such operations should be avoided in general to allow a better assessment of the financial soundness of the state-owned commercial banks and minimize potential adverse macro-financial implications for the economy.

25. Implications for the financial sector of the sanctioning of four small banks appear limited. The NBKR has appropriately intensified its on-site inspections, while other commercial banks have responded by closer scrutiny of their lending decisions.

![](images/88ec38a12b2d14801c29982fcf05edd128d68bf2f77aca4a60a852b85fa175b4.jpg)

![](images/c81bcf3ccb07139dede7fb770687a1bd892c756c3c23adab4cea0340cffad8e5.jpg)

26. The fast-growing virtual assets sector, cross-border payments and trade pose new risks, requiring strengthened mitigation measures (Annex VII). The AML/CFT regulatory framework for VASPs is being strengthened, notably expanding the licensing requirements to the beneficial owners of VASPs, which should be followed by a comprehensive review of the current license-holders to ensure absence of criminal control in the sector. $^{15}$ Strengthening corporate transparency and screening of cross-border financial flows is crucial to mitigate financial integrity risks. To ensure sustainable development of cross-border trade, risk-based post-clearance

monitoring of controlled goods should be enhanced to address re-export and diversion risks. While the amount of issuance of the USDKG stablecoin is limited, additional issuances of stablecoins could undermine the NBKR's ability to conduct monetary policy. $^{16}$

## Authorities' Views

27. The authorities share staff's concerns about rapid credit growth but stress that their supervisory efforts are adequate to the task. They agree about the need to monitor the development of virtual assets in the banking sector and develop a sound regulatory response with clear institutional mandates – for banking sector stability and to limit AML/CFT concerns.

## D. Structural Reforms

## 28. Multi-pronged structural reforms are essential for sustainable growth.

\- Business environment. The authorities would be encouraged to reduce the footprint of the state in the economy by focusing more on creating an enabling environment for the private sector and less on direct interventions through SOEs. Decisive measures are needed to level the playing field for the private sector and reduce barriers to entry for potential competitors. Among the priorities are advancing tariff reforms to improve cost recovery in energy SOEs, strengthening financial performance oversight to identify emerging fiscal risks, and enhancing SOE management and transparency—and measures to promote competition and bolster private sector development. $^{17}$

\- Governance. Addressing macro-critical governance weaknesses is needed to avert macro-financial risks and spur growth. The authorities should continue implementing the 2025-2030 Anti-Corruption Strategy and strengthening the rule of law by developing the integrity vetting of judicial candidates. The asset disclosure by public officials should be enhanced by expanding the scope of published information and strengthening the verification procedures.

\- AI. Labor market exposure to generative AI is currently moderate, due in large part to the economy's relative concentration in low-automation activities in the informal sector, limiting both the risks and the potential gains. Addressing the constraints to AI adoption (such as gaps in digital infrastructure and in human capital) will require reforms in digital infrastructure, workforce skills, governance/legal frameworks and data systems (see the forthcoming annex on Measuring AI Preparedness of the Kyrgyz Republic).

![](images/e93e7f89a4d708c994571cd937175beb98a199bc31aaa93338343add06291200.jpg)

29. Reducing informality would foster inclusive growth. Establishing an unemployment insurance system—designed to operate countercyclically—would reduce reliance on informal work as a buffer against economic shocks. Complementary measures, including vocational training, job search assistance, and job placement services would improve skill matching and labor mobility—hence, supporting sustainable integration into formal employment. Expanding SMEs' access to finance would facilitate their transition to the formal sector (see forthcoming SIP on Informal Employment in the Kyrgyz Republic).

## Authorities' Views

30. The authorities view structural reforms as key to sustaining growth and protecting the most vulnerable. They stress their commitment to reducing informality, including to benefit from the potentially transformative impact of AI. The authorities aim to leverage AI technologies to enhance public service delivery and support broader socio-economic development. To this end, the authorities plan to continue strengthening the regulatory framework and investing further in digital infrastructure. They view the implementation of their 2025 anti-corruption strategy as an essential part of effective economic regulation. The authorities acknowledge the concerns about SOE competitiveness, but note the improved monitoring of associated risks.

## E. Data Adequacy

31. Data quality is broadly adequate for surveillance, but there is room for improvement. In particular, large errors and omissions in the balance of payments remain and continue to complicate staff analysis. National accounts statistics could also be improved to minimize frequent large revisions. The IMF stands ready to provide technical assistance in these areas.

## STAFF APPRAISAL

32. The Kyrgyz Republic is transitioning from its low-income status. The expansion of external trade, robust remittances and capital inflows, and strong construction activity supported by government spending have underpinned rapid growth of recent years, contributing to rising incomes and declining poverty. At the same time, stronger domestic demand pressures and external factors have elevated inflation above the NBKR's target range. In the context of rapid credit growth, strong wage increases and elevated liquidity, this points to overheating that requires a timely policy response.

33. Creating fiscal space is essential to support priority infrastructure and social spending while containing domestic demand. Additional revenue can be mobilized by streamlining VAT exemptions and special tax regimes, increasing PIT progressivity, and strengthening revenue administration.

34. Stronger public financial management (PFM) measures are needed to enhance the efficiency and transparency of public spending. Measures should focus on containing the public wage bill, gradually reducing energy subsidies, improving the targeting of social spending, and strengthening public investment management to enhance efficiency and resilience.

35. To further reinforce fiscal sustainability and policy credibility, prudent debt management, avoidance of quasi-fiscal financing, and stronger fiscal risk monitoring—especially related to SOEs—are critical. Continued efforts to enhance fiscal transparency, including comprehensive coverage of all public sector operations, would help ensure that fiscal risks are fully identified and effectively managed. In this context, the Stabilization Fund should either be dissolved or fully incorporated into the fiscal perimeter to ensure its operations are transparently reflected in fiscal reporting and risk assessments.

36. Monetary policy should remain focused on returning inflation to the NBKR's target range and anchoring expectations. With external pressures and strong domestic demand keeping inflation elevated, the recent policy rate increase is appropriate. Given persistent excess liquidity and the weak monetary policy transmission mechanism, however, strengthening the operational framework—through improved liquidity management, a narrower and more symmetric corridor, and reduced subsidized lending via state-owned banks—is essential.

Repeated profit transfers to the budget, while capital remains below statutory thresholds, risk undermining the effectiveness of monetary policy. The authorities should adhere to the central bank law and suspend regular profit transfers until capital is restored, while ensuring that policy decisions remain fully aligned with the price stability mandate.

38. Greater exchange rate flexibility and more diversified reserves would enhance resilience. Although reserves have risen due to gold revaluation, high concentration in gold increases exposure to price volatility. Continued diversification and conversion of eligible gold into monetary gold would strengthen external resilience and reserve management.

## 39. The financial sector requires strengthened supervisory capacity and risk monitoring.

Risk-based supervision should be strengthened; directed lending should be curtailed; and proactive macroprudential measures to safeguard financial stability and resilience should be considered. Efforts to enhance AML/CFT effectiveness should continue, including operationalizing the beneficial ownership registry and assessing virtual asset risks. Priorities include stronger supervision and governance of VASPs, alignment with international standards, robust financial stability safeguards, consumer protection, mitigation of illicit finance risks, and monitoring trade in controlled goods.

## 40. Structural reforms remain crucial to enhancing sustainable and inclusive growth.

Priorities include advancing SOE reform, improving the business environment and competition, strengthening the rule of law and anti-corruption efforts, and addressing informality and labor market rigidities. Continued investment in human capital and digital infrastructure, alongside improvements in governance and labor market policies, will be important to harness the gains from digitalization and AI adoption.

41. Strengthening the quality of macroeconomic statistics is essential, particularly in the areas of external sector statistics and national accounts statistics. Improved coverage, consistency, and transparency in these datasets would enhance policy analysis, support more accurate risk assessment, and strengthen overall macroeconomic surveillance.

42. Staff recommends that the next Article IV consultation be held on a standard 12-month cycle.

Sources: country authorities and IMF staff estimates.

## Annex III. External Sector Assessment

Overall Assessment: The external position of the Kyrgyz Republic in 2025 is assessed to remain substantially weaker than the level implied by medium-term fundamentals and desirable policies. For this assessment, official Balance of Payments (BoP) data for 2025 reflect the actual data, including large chronic errors and omissions, for the first half of the year. The officially reported current account has therefore been adjusted to account for the portion of errors and omissions, which, in the view of staff, reflect large unrecorded re-exports to Russia. The current account gap and the associated overvaluation reflect strong domestic demand for imports. Gross official foreign exchange reserves exceed the IMF reserve adequacy metric for resource-constrained economies, but they are projected to decline over the medium term.

Potential Policy Responses: Maintain exchange rate flexibility in the presence of inadequacies in BOP data; implement reforms to strengthen competitiveness and reduce procyclical fiscal stimulus, with a view to reducing external sector imbalances and aligning the external sector with fundamentals in the medium term.

## Foreign Assets and Liabilities: Position and Trajectory

1. Background: The Net International Investment Position (NIIP) has gradually improved since 2024 as a percentage of GDP, thanks in large part to the high nominal GDP growth. It improved significantly from -38 percent of GDP in 2024 to -30 percent of GDP in 2025. Gross foreign assets declined slightly from 56 percent of GDP in 2024 to 54 percent of GDP, owing to exceptional GDP growth in 2025, and despite a sharp increase in international reserves (by 28.8 percent in nominal US\$ terms). Gross foreign liabilities fell from 94 percent of GDP to 83 percent, also due to high nominal GDP growth (external public and publicly guaranteed debt, trade credit and advances, and foreign direct investment liabilities of around 33, 11, and 30 percent of GDP, respectively).

2. Assessment: The less negative NIIP does not pose immediate risks to external stability. In the medium term, concessional external public debt coupled with limited portfolio capital inflows largely mitigate the external risks. The improvements began in 2022, following a period of relative stability in the NIIP over the preceding five years.

<table><tr><td>2025</td><td>NIIP</td><td>Gross Assets</td><td>Debt Assets</td><td>Gross Liab.</td><td>Debt Liab.</td></tr><tr><td>(Percent of GDP)</td><td>-30</td><td>54</td><td>1.0</td><td>83</td><td>33</td></tr></table>

## Current Account—Background

3. The officially recorded current account deficit is projected to decline to 20.3 percent of GDP in 2025 from 25.3 percent in 2024. The 2025 current account deficit reflects a half year of chronically large errors and omissions of around 14 percent, whereas the 2024 current account reflects actual errors and omissions for the full year, amounting to just under 17 percent of GDP. While high, they reflect an improvement on the errors and omissions of more than 40 percent of GDP in the official published 2023 current account. Imports are estimated to have stabilized in 2025, as the re-export trade matured following three years of heady growth. The 2025 estimated CA

deficit reflects higher modeled gold exports, strong net inflows of remittances of over 14 percent of GDP despite a slight decline since the start of Russia's war in Ukraine, and very strong real GDP growth of 11.1 percent. Overall, staff estimates that the true current account deficit for 2025, adjusted for errors and omissions that reflect unrecorded re-exports, has remained stable at around 7.5 percent of GDP relative to the true current account deficit for 2024.

4. To assess the external position of the Kyrgyz Republic, staff adjusted the official current account using its own estimate of re-exports. Imports of consumer goods increased further in 2025, underscoring the gap between these imports and potential absorption even with strong growth and after accounting for additional consumption by migrants. As in the previous ESA, staff concludes that a large part of the import increase was re-exported to the Eurasian Customs Union (EACU), $^{1}$ mainly to Russia, but were unrecorded because intra-EACU trade is not adequately monitored by statistic agencies in the absence of customs declarations for intra-EACA trade. These unrecorded exports, reflected in large positive errors and omissions, are estimated at 11.8 percent of GDP in 2025, which would imply a CA deficit of 8.4 percent of GDP. In the medium term, the CA deficit is projected to remain relatively high at about 7 percent of GDP by 2031 – mainly due to imports related to Kambarata HPP and the China-Uzbekistan-Kyrgyzstan Railway.

## Current Account—Assessment

5. The Current Account (CA) model identifies a negative current account gap of 4.1 percent of GDP implying an overvaluation of the som by 11.4 percent (Text Table). This CA gap reflects adjustments for cyclical factors (output gap), the impact of the war in Ukraine on trade, and a policy gap of 2.3 percent of GDP. The policy gap is largely due to better fiscal outcomes compared to its desired level and the rest of the world.

6. Using the Current Account model, staff assesses the external position of the Kyrgyz Republic in 2025 to be substantially weaker than the level implied by medium-term fundamentals and desirable policies. If adjusted for the shock of the war in Ukraine, the 2025 CA deficit would narrow to 8.4 percent of GDP, consistent with a negative CA gap of over 4 percent of GDP and an overvaluation of the som by 11 percent.

## Real Exchange Rate

7. The Kyrgyz som depreciated by 2.6 percent against the USD during 2025. It experienced little volatility throughout the year. The real effective exchange rate (REER) depreciated marginally during 2025, largely reflecting the inflation differential between the Kyrgyz Republic and its trading partners, and the limited appreciation of the Kyrgyz Som in nominal effective terms.

## 8. The REER model identifies a small positive current account gap of 1.7 percent, implying a small undervaluation of the som

![](images/f3b4e92e3c3599cc1b4acb0dffbf0770b4c54074bf8aaa70a94d7910f9d3c1aa.jpg)

by 4.7 percent. The implied undervaluation of the som with the REER model is at odds with the implied overvaluation of the som in the CA model.

9. Staff puts more weight on the results of the CA model, for two reasons. First, the REER-norm and the REER fitted values from the REER model are higher than the actual REER, implying a need for appreciation to close the gap. Second, in the REER model, the main cause of the undervaluation remains the interest rate differential with the rest of the world, implying that the model results are driven by the capital and financial accounts, which are considerably smaller than the CA balance in the Kyrgyz Republic. Looking forward, sustaining strong growth with limited inflation beyond the boom of 2022-2025 will require an alignment of the external sector with its fundamentals, which in turn will require a prudent management of reserves and reforms to strengthen competitiveness.

## Capital and Financial Accounts: Flows and Policy Measures

10. Background. In 2025, the capital and financial account balance (excluding changes in reserves) is estimated to have improved to 7.7 percent of GDP from 7.4 percent in 2024, driven by small increases in net Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) under the financial account and net official flows under the capital account.

11. Assessment. The Kyrgyz Republic remains potentially vulnerable to sudden shifts in the global and regional financial conditions, especially as regards Russia, which is its main export market and the source of most remittances. Efforts to secure more concessional financing and implementation of reforms consistent with regional or global integration and resulting in higher FDI inflows will be important to mitigate these vulnerabilities over the medium term.

## FX Intervention and Reserves Level

12. Background. While the NBKR bought and sold FX in 2024, it intervened in the FX market in 2025 only to sell the equivalent of \$672.9 million in FX, with 32 percent and 43 percent of all sales conducted in Q2 and Q4 over a few daily operations. These sales aimed at mopping up the excess liquidity injected by the NBKR's ongoing purchase of domestically produced gold, and more generally to contain the growing inflationary pressures. The NBKR converted non-monetary gold to monetary gold worth \$3.4 billion at end-2025, which more than offset the impact of FX sales on international reserves. As a result, and with the additional boost of the proceeds of the Eurobond issuance of \$0.7 billion, reserves increased by more than \$3.6 billion from 4 months of prospective imports (including re-exports) in 2024 to over 6.3 months in 2025. Gross reserves are projected to decline gradually to 5.6 months in 2026, and further to 2 months by 2031 (after the Eurobond bullet repayment), which is below the IMF's reserve adequacy metrics of 4.5 months for credit-constrained economies.

13. Assessment. The NBKR is still holding a stock of non-monetary gold (equivalent to over \$1.3 billion at end-December 2025 valued at historic purchase prices), which if converted to monetary gold would further strengthen NBKR's international reserves. With the conversion of the existing stock of non-monetary to monetary gold, gross reserves would increase by around 2 months of import of goods and services in 2026, and gradually decline to around 4 months by 2031.

Annex IV. Data Issues

The top cell for &quot;Granularity&quot; of Monetary and Financial Statistics shows staff&#x27;s assessment of the granularity of the reported Monetary and Financial Statistics data, while the bottom cell shows that of the Financial Soundness indicators.</td></tr><tr><td>A</td><td colspan="7">The data provided to the Fund are adequate for surveillance.</td></tr><tr><td>B</td><td colspan="7">The data provided to the Fund have some shortcomings but are broadly adequate for surveillance.</td></tr><tr><td>C</td><td colspan="7">The data provided to the Fund have some shortcomings that somewhat hamper surveillance.</td></tr><tr><td>D</td><td colspan="7">The data provided to the Fund have serious shortcomings that significantly hamper surveillance.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="8">Rationale for staff assessment. Data weaknesses significantly impact the assessment of macroeconomic and financial risks in the Kyrgyz Republic, particularly in national accounts, fiscal and external sector statistics. A major issue is the large volume of unrecorded re-exports, leading to external sector discrepancies with errors and omissions reaching more than 25 percent of GDP in recent years. This distortion undermines the reliability of external balances, yet the authorities have not taken corrective measures. Additionally, it creates inconsistencies between the expenditure and production approaches in GDP measurement, further complicating economic analysis. Recent national accounts revisions and limited gold-related data disclosure have introduced additional concerns, suggesting possible misclassification or measurement issues. On the fiscal side, the use of an extra-budgetary fund undermines the monitoring of effective expenditures and the reconciliation of accounts between the NBKR and the Treasury. These data weaknesses compromise the accuracy of risk assessments and policy recommendations, increasing the likelihood of ineffective macroeconomic and financial policies. Addressing these challenges through improved statistical methodologies and greater transparency is essential for informed policymaking.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="8">Changes since the last Article IV consultation The last Article IV consultation highlighted concerns about external sector statistics, particularly the significant errors and omissions. However, no improvements have been made since then, and the issue remains unresolved. Additionally, the challenges related to national accounts revisions have emerged after the last Article IV, further complicating macroeconomic analysis. Moreover, the use of extra-budgetary funds undermines the quality of treasury data. These unresolved data weaknesses continue to hinder the accuracy of economic assessments and policy recommendations.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="8">Corrective actions and capacity development priorities Staff reiterated its offer to provide technical assistance (TA) to improve external sector statistics; however, the authorities have not yet agreed to receive this support. On national accounts, the authorities, supported by CCAMTAC, are working to develop and publish discrete quarterly GDP estimates.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="8">Use of data and/or estimates in Article IV consultations in lieu of official statistics available to staff. Staff relied on the official data for the actual years. However, re-exports are calculated and adjusted in the balance of payment projections.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="8">Other data gaps. The Kyrgyz Republic would benefit from new income and gender inequality data.</td></tr></table>

# Annex V. Assessing Fiscal Risks in the Public Pension System of the Kyrgyz Republic $^{1}$

The purpose of this analysis is to assess the sustainability and adequacy of the old-age Public Pension System (PPS) in the Kyrgyz Republic in light of the international experience; identify its main drivers and policy variables; analyze the projected fiscal impact of the PPS of specific demographic, economic or policy stresses; and discuss considerations to help maintain fiscal sustainability. The results of the modeling exercise show that the PPS faces sustainability issues exacerbated by risks in the medium term.

## A. Background and Rationale

1. The PPS is an important mechanism to redistribute income and combat poverty in old age. The Social Fund (SF) is the autonomous public agency with rank of ministry responsible for social welfare payments and all pension payments, the latter accounting for 85 percent of its budget in 20225.

2. The old-age PPS operates de facto as a modified “pay-as-you-go” (PAYGO) “defined benefits” (DB) scheme. The effective DB in the PPS is a percentage of the last salary in active service, on top of a basic flat rate benefit. $^{2}$ Retirees in the PPS are funded in part by a PAYGO contribution applicable to formal sector employees and employers, with an effective rate of 17 percent of the salary. The rest is financed by a transfer from the state budget.

3. SF accounts have been broadly balanced over the past 5 years. Overall SF expenditures in recent years have been around 7.5 percent of GDP, of which 5.5 percentage points are for the PPS, and around 1 percentage point for overhead administrative costs. On the revenue side, the contributions to the PPS amount to 4.5 percent of GDP, with the rest of SF revenue covered by the state budget.

4. The fundamentals of the PPS and recent policy shifts raise questions about its sustainability and adequacy. First, while in 2025 there were around 100,000 retirees who were contributors to the PAYGO scheme, there were overall around 700,000 beneficiaries of old-age pensions. This means that with roughly 1 million current contributors, the dependency ratio of around 10 percent for formal sector retirees becomes an effective ratio of 70 percent to account for all beneficiaries of the PPS. The OECD invites vigilance if this ratio exceeds 30. Second, while real GDP growth was over 10 percent on average for the past three years, the SF revenue base did not exhibit an equivalent growth. Third, in an effort to entice firms into the formal economy, the Government has slashed the PAYGO contributory rate by 10 percentage points for new adherents, thus undermining the earmarked revenue base and increasing the burden on the budget. And

fourth, the overhead costs remain high at 12 percent of SF expenditure, even though they have decreased over the past 5 years.

## B. Comparative Landscape of Central Asian Pension Systems

5. A PAYGO framework remains the dominant pension model across the Central Asia region. The Kyrgyz Republic, together with Tajikistan, Turkmenistan, and Uzbekistan has conducted several reforms to modify their Soviet-era pension model characterized by a PAYGO DB system. One exception is Kazakhstan, which has transitioned to a fully funded defined contribution system. Statutory retirement ages remain relatively low in the region - particularly for women - while contribution and working requirements differ markedly. Minimum qualifying periods are longest in Tajikistan and Uzbekistan (25 years for men, 20 for women), whereas it is currently 6 years in the Kyrgyz Republic, where it is projected to increase to 20 years by 2045. Contribution rates and the balance of employee-employer burden vary widely, with Kazakhstan featuring the most employee-based model (10 percent contribution), while Tajikistan, Turkmenistan, Uzbekistan rely almost entirely on employer contributions. In the Kyrgyz Republic, the contribution to the PAYGO component by the employee is about 8 percent and 15 percent for the public service and large private employers (lowered to 1 percent for most of small and medium enterprises).

6. Despite a generally favorable demographic profile across Central Asia, the Kyrgyz Republic faces disproportionate fiscal pressure due to its high pension expenditures. Among the four countries with a PAYGO system, the Kyrgyz Republic and Uzbekistan are those with higher formal old-age dependency ratio $^{3}$ of 9-10 compared to 6-7 percent in Tajikistan and Turkmenistan (Figure 1). The old-age pension expenditure of Kyrgyzstan is also highest among the Central Asian countries, reaching 6.7 percent of GDP, while other countries spend between 3-4.5 percent of GDP (Figure 2).

## C. Modeling of the Public Pension System

## Methodology

7. The analysis focuses on the FYSG DB public pensions, using a simple model developed by Fund staff. The model comprises three major clusters of variables by gender: (i) demographic variables (population growth, mortality); (ii) economic variables (GDP growth proxied by headcount growth, inflation proxied by indexation, structure and distribution of wages, promotion, attrition); and (iii) PPS specifications (number of contributors, pensioners, initial pension amount, defined benefit policy, partial pensions for early retirees, contribution policy, etc.).

8. Based on the preceding inputs, the model generates PPS projections. They include (i) PPS demographics over time (number of contributors and pensioners, dependency ratio, life expectancy); (ii) PPS benefits and finances (wage bill, pensions, PPS revenues, PPS current balance, etc.).

## Calibration

9. The model was populated starting in base year 1996 to approximate the main elements of the PPS financial accounts and demographics at steady state, in 2025. Specifically, in 2025 PAYGO DB pension outlays are around 110 billion soms, and PAYGO contributions are 83 billion soms. Overhead costs are 10 billion soms. Other demographic and economic data for 2025 were drawn from official Kyrgyz statistics: formal sector employees estimated at around 1 million evenly split between the private sector and the State (out of a population of 7.3 billion), an average annual wage economy-wide is 500,000 soms, 700,000 retirees drawing old age pensions averaging 140,000 soms. The pension age is 63 for men and 58 for women, and both genders start work at 18 years old.

10. The main stylized modeling assumptions for the baseline scenario reflect recent economic developments and policy decisions, broadly in line with the macroeconomic framework (See text table 2). Higher economic growth justifies a higher headcount growth (4 percent, up from 2 percent) and higher inflation starting in 2025 justifies higher indexation; the decrease of the contribution rate by 10 percentage points also justifies higher headcount growth in formal employment, and, conversely, a gradual lowering of the number of new non-contributing retirees.

## D. Outlook under the Baseline Scenario

11. The demographics of the PPS are concerning, but stand to improve under a baseline scenario. The effective dependency ratio, i.e. total pensioners as shared of formal employment, is 70 percent in 2025, but improves to around 36 percent by 2065. This is due to a relatively fast growth of the labor force in the formal sector, while the number of pensioners is constrained by old age and a more limited accrual of beneficiaries who did not contribute.

12. The financials of the PPS are under strain. Over the next ten years, the decreasing contribution rate will cause the deficit of the PPS to increase to over 3.3 percent of GDP by 2035. Overall contributions fall from 4.3 to 1.5 percent of GDP by 2035, while outlays would fall from 6.4 to 4.8 percent of GDP. The PPS shows a steadily narrowing deficit thereafter – down to 0.5 percent of GDP by 2065 - as headcount growth for the workforce becomes the dominant driver of PPS dynamics, and as the impact of the more limited increase of non-contributing retirees plays out.

13. The sustainability of the pension system will depend on vigilance, judicious PPS policies and favorable economic and demographic circumstances. PPS policies should target adequate contributions from the workforce, tight control over pension eligibility, control of overhead costs and annual pension increases no higher than inflation. An auspicious socio-economic environment will help contain the challenges of the PPS, since real GDP is projected to increase faster than headcount in formal employment. The wage bill of the formal sector (public and private) therefore halves over 40 years as the economy grows through more capital-intensive sectors.

Figure 3. Kyrgyz Republic: Select Indicators for the Modeled Public Pension System  
![](images/72f4cf4b16c833f2204d27b76ec6f1b9b55d27cecfc9baa9f9650925a20b1114.jpg)

![](images/5280b9f6c771a774615fb47ecdf60031d62e9b2fbdf29d3357323d4fb1910f04.jpg)

14. The payments for retirees under the PPS raise concerns about the standard of living in old age. The average pension is only one third of the average wage in 2025. Moreover, average pensions do not track inflation fully over the long term. The lowest modeled pension of 78,000 soms for 2025 is higher than the national poverty line set at 65,417 soms per capita per year in 2024, but is inadequate to support a dependent of the retiree.

## E. Challenges Facing the PPS

## 15. Specific stress tests indicate that the PPS is highly sensitive to economic and demographic

developments. Moreover, the effects of such developments are difficult to counteract rapidly with PPS-specific policy measures. In an alternative scenario where the baseline is adjusted to reflect a lower real economic growth rate of 3 percent instead of 5, and a lower workforce headcount growth of 2 percent instead of 4, the overall balance of the PPS adopts a different profile. The deficit is 4 percent of GDP in 2035, but it falls more slowly afterwards, remaining at 1.8 percent of GDP by 2065.

Figure 4. Kyrgyz Republic: PPS - Alternative Scenario  
![](images/e11a44a31f97407895659c7fd4b62f2e85f002897ebfd4f6579acd57ba1e290a.jpg)

## F. Policy Considerations

16. Continuous monitoring and transparent reporting of PPS finances alongside the annual budget are essential. Given long adjustment lags, early identification of adverse trends is critical to avoid abrupt and procyclical fiscal corrections.

17. Policy changes affecting contributions or benefits should be gradual and well-calibrated. While measures to support formalization are important, permanent reductions in contribution rates should be weighed carefully against their fiscal costs and, where possible, offset by broadening the contribution base or strengthening compliance.

18. Critical ratios should be monitored diligently. Allowing overhead costs to increase at the same rate as nominal GDP, loosening eligibility criteria for old-age pensions, or allowing pension indexation in excess of inflation could put the PPS on an accelerated path to unsustainability. Conversely, maintaining indexation broadly in line with inflation and containing administrative spending can deliver sizable long-term savings

19. Contingency measures should be pre-designed and modelled in advance to address evolving economic and demographic developments. Options such as gradual increases in statutory retirement ages—particularly in response to rising longevity—and restrictions on eligibility for non-contributors can significantly improve balances, but only if implemented early.

## Annex VI. Kyrgyz Republic: Artificial Intelligence Exposure and Preparedness $^{1}$

## A. Introduction

1. Artificial intelligence (AI) has immense potential to boost productivity and strengthen public service delivery. For the Kyrgyz Republic, AI could serve as a catalyst for structural transformation given the country's modest productivity growth over the last decade and large inefficiencies in public spending (Rao and others, 2025 and IMF's Spending Efficiency Database, 2025). Nevertheless, realizing these gains is contingent on complementary investments in infrastructure, skills, and institutions. At the same time, AI adoption carries risks of labor market disruption and widening inequality if left unmanaged (Brollo and others, 2024).

2. The Kyrgyz Republic's National Development Program (NDP) 2030 articulates an objective to leverage AI as a cross-cutting instrument for the country's long-term modernization agenda. Spanning education, healthcare, agriculture, industry, and public financial management, the NDP envisions AI-driven improvements in service delivery and productivity, underpinned by investments in digital infrastructure and AI competencies through STEM-focused programs.

3. Against this backdrop, the annex assesses the Kyrgyz Republic's AI exposure as well as its preparedness to manage the transition and broaden the gains of AI. First, it examines the extent to which the labor market is exposed to AI as well as the current AI adoption in the economy. Second, it evaluates the country's AI preparedness using the IMF's AI Preparedness Index (AIPI) framework, encompassing four main dimensions—digital infrastructure, human capital and labor market policies, innovation and economic integration, and legal frameworks. The last section concludes with policy recommendations.

## B. Assessing the Potential Exposure of Jobs to Generative AI

4. We construct a novel measure of exposure of jobs to generative AI in the Kyrgyz Republic. The AI exposure score is derived from task-based methodologies (Gmyrek and others, 2023), reflecting “potential exposure” to GenAI and not the actual impact. Each of the tasks within an occupation received a 0-1 potential automation score, with 0 indicating it is not possible and 1 indicating it was entirely possible to perform the task with Generative AI. There are six levels of exposure, ranging from highest exposure, where most tasks can be automated, to minimal or no exposure, where automation potential is limited. Using the Labor Force Survey data of International Labor Organization (ILO) for occupations at 2-digit ISCO level, it assesses the proportions of existing jobs that could be potentially automated by the generative AI.

Source: Microsoft (2026)

5. The share of employment exposed to generative AI in the Kyrgyz Republic is estimated to be moderate relative to the world average. About 7 percent of employment in the Kyrgyz Republic falls into one of the three highest exposure gradients (from moderate and above), compared to 13 percent in lower-middle income peers, 16 percent as the world average, and 28 percent in high income countries (Figure 1, Panel 1). In line with ILO (2025), a larger share of female employment in the Kyrgyz Republic is subject to top three exposure gradients, reflecting women's concentration in data processing and routine cognitive tasks (Figure 1, Panel 2).

## 6. Employment in the public sector has a larger potential exposure to generative AI. As of

employment is in the public sector. Of these, 14 percent of jobs are in top three exposure risks, which is double the corresponding level for total employment (Figure 1, Panel 3), due to their concentration in administrative, data-processing, and cognitive tasks.

## 7. The low aggregate AI exposure in the Kyrgyz Republic is driven by a large share of informal

![](images/a4c6611068c45bfe3722c0b3905e41fefd5936e3c75114775da55b916158fc83.jpg)

employment. The informal sector accounts for 57.5 percent of employment, with significant concentration in agriculture, construction, and manufacturing (Muratkhanov and Talishi, 2026). The tasks associated with these jobs are more physical and less cognitive, thus mitigating the near-term risks of replacement by generative AI. However, high informality may slow technology diffusion and limit productivity spillovers, reinforcing labor market duality.

![](images/11bbef9c2edab94ec40c94d0ca521ab6f1bcc66a02d44bf129b8f7cdde189617.jpg)  
Figure 3. Kyrgyz Republic: AI Preparedness  
Source: IMF's AIPi Database, Cazzaniga and others (2024), Microsoft (2026), and IMF staff calculation.

8. Nevertheless, the concentration of employment in sectors with limited direct AI task exposure suggests that near-term productivity gains from AI could be limited. About 93 percent of employment in the Kyrgyz Republic has minimal or no exposure to generative AI—significantly above the global average of 76 percent—reflecting the economy's sectoral composition, where AI task substitution and augmentation potential remain limited. This is reinforced by a low AI diffusion of 8 percent in 2025 in the Kyrgyz Republic, measured as the share of people who have used a generative AI product (Figure 2). This places the Kyrgyz Republic in the middle of CCA range countries, but well below top CCA countries, the global average, and far behind the top 10 average. Notably, the improvement between H1 and H2 2025 was only half the global average pace and less than a quarter of the top 10 rate, suggesting the diffusion gap is widening rather than closing.

## C. Evaluating AI Preparedness

9. Measuring and evaluating AI preparedness can help identify gaps and prioritize policy actions needed to maximize the benefits of AI adoption. The IMF AI Preparedness Index (AIPI), developed by Cazzaniga and others (2024), assesses 174 economies across four pillars—digital infrastructure, human capital and labor market policies (HC&LMP), innovation and economic integration, and regulation and ethics—providing a structured framework to benchmark countries against peers and identify priority reform areas.

10. The Kyrgyz Republic's AI preparedness score falls between the LIC and EME average, placing it in the bottom half of the CCA group. As noted in Cazzaniga and others (2024), wealthier economies are generally better prepared than low-income countries to adopt AI. The score of the Kyrgyz Republic is 0.43, below the EME average of 0.46 and well below the regional leaders Kazakhstan (0.55) and Georgia (0.53), reflecting weaker digital infrastructure and a less digitally skilled labor force, among other factors (Figure 3, Panel 1). Low AI preparedness is in turn associated with low AI adoption, limiting the scope for productivity gains (Figure 3, Panel 2).

11. Digital infrastructure represents a structural impediment to technological diffusion in the Kyrgyz Republic. Digital infrastructure is one of the two foundational dimensions of AI preparedness, besides HC&LMP. While this measure is slightly higher than the average Middle East and Central Asia (MECA), it lags behind the corresponding measure in top EME and Top CCA (Figure 4), mostly reflecting limited secure internet servers as well as public sector's online service infrastructure and private sector's e-commerce business environment (Figure 5, Panel 1).

## 12. Significant gaps also exist

regarding HC&LMP. Despite relatively high public spending on education in the region, poor education quality—reflected in a narrow graduate skillset and below-average STEM graduation rates—limits human capital development (Figures 4 and 5, Panel 2). Moreover, the weak link between productivity gains and wage growth reduces workers' incentives to adopt and leverage AI tools to enhance efficiency. Weak social protection and underdeveloped active labor market policies (ALMPs) could further

Figure 4. Kyrgyz Republic: AI Preparedness  
![](images/bccbe7e7818ee4a2ebc074a82396b83ab8a02e443396daf21610c8cf326db006.jpg)  
Source: Cazzaniga and others (2024)

prolong the transition, given the absence of adequate income support and limited retraining and skills-matching capacity — both critical for helping workers adapt to AI-driven labor market changes (Figure 5, Panel 3).

![](images/a60b2540066a1ed1c7f1f46b0eb4a1f477e5134ef608029bb372097396917b63.jpg)  
Source: Cazzaniga and others (2024)

## 13. Beyond foundational preparedness, there are gaps in innovation and regulatory

framework in the Kyrgyz Republic. The gaps in these aspects align with the average MECA but lie well below regional and EME leaders (Figure 4). Strengthening innovation capacity and regulatory frameworks would further support AI adoption; however, these efforts will be less effective without prior improvements in digital infrastructure and workforce skills, underscoring the importance of policy sequencing.

## D. Conclusions and Policy Recommendations

14. To reap the productivity benefits of AI while managing the associated risks, the Kyrgyz Republic needs a coherent policy framework that addresses its structural preparedness gaps. While aggregate labor market exposure to generative AI is moderate—reflecting the economy's large informal sector and concentration in low-automation activities—the small share of AI-exposed employment and low AI diffusion could limit the productivity gains that AI could otherwise deliver. At the same time, the country's AI preparedness score places it in the bottom half of the CCA group, with structural weaknesses across all four AIPi pillars, particularly digital infrastructure and human capital and labor market policies—constraining the economy's ability to absorb and benefit from AI technologies.

## 15. Addressing these gaps will require targeted, sequenced policy actions across several fronts, prioritizing digital infrastructure and human capital:

\- Strengthen digital infrastructure and AI diffusion. Expanding affordable high quality broadband connectivity, improving public digital services, and supporting broader access to AI tools are important for closing the diffusion gap and enabling economy-wide productivity gains.

\- Improve human capital quality and labor market adaptability. Enhancing education efficiency, including through better STEM outcomes, digital skills, and AI literacy, while strengthening ALMPs and social protection to support workers displaced by AI-driven structural change, is essential to ensure the gains from AI adoption are broadly shared.

\- Build an enabling environment for AI adoption. Strengthening innovation ecosystems and regulatory frameworks could support AI adoption by enhancing firms' capacity to develop, access, and adapt global AI technologies, while improving governance and trust in digital tools—thereby encouraging private investment in digital innovation.

\- Enhance data foundations for evidence-based AI policy. Integrating technology-use modules into the national labor force survey would fill a critical gap in monitoring AI exposure and adoption at the firm and worker level — providing the empirical basis for policy adjustment as AI deployment evolves.
