## Annex VII. Mitigation of Cross-border Financial Integrity Risks in Kyrgyz Republic

## A. Money Laundering/Terrorist Financing (ML/TF) Risks and Country Context

1. Kyrgyz Republic is facing material cross-border ML/TF risks due to its geographic location and jurisdictional vulnerabilities. The 2025 National Risk Assessment (NRA) $^{1}$ identified fraud, tax evasion, and corruption as the highest-threat predicate offenses for ML, followed by drug trafficking, customs offenses, and organized crime. Recognizing the importance of transnational crime and ML, the NRA usefully included an in-depth analysis of cross-border risks and typologies. It highlights the Kyrgyz Republic's role as the regional transportation hub and a transit link between China and Russia as well as geographic position along drug trafficking routes with associated financial integrity risks. Corruption remains both an offense generating significant criminal proceeds as well as structural vulnerability, enabling ML and other economic crimes. High-risk cross-border typologies identified include placement and withdrawal of cash from bank accounts, "transit" wire transfers between accounts of legal persons and complex layering schemes involving multiple jurisdictions.

2. Misuse of companies registered in Kyrgyz Republic represents a critical structural vulnerability identified in the NRA. The assessment highlights the mass registration of corporate entities, with more than 65 percent of registered companies conducting no economic activity, which suggests exposure to risks of corporate misuse to facilitate ML. The high share of companies with non-resident participation, particularly in high-risk sectors such as trade, construction, and consulting, creates opportunities for beneficial ownership concealment and integration of illicit proceeds into the formal economy. The NRA specifically notes high risks associated with misuse of offshore companies, corporate nominees, and proxies.

## B. Virtual Asset Service Providers (VASPs): Regulatory and Supervisory Framework

3. The value of transactions conducted through the VASPs increased threefold in 2025, exceeding the GDP, while the number of VASPs has grown to 188. The transactions via VASPs increased from USD 2.5 bln. in 2023 to 31.3 bln. in 2025, reaching approximately 138 percent of GDP in 2025, as the Kyrgyz Republic became one of the world's fast growing VASP jurisdictions relative to the size of the financial sector and economy. $^{2}$ As the number of transactions via VASPs fell by one-third in 2025, the average transaction value increased four-fold to USD 14 573. In 2023, the average transaction value reached USD 120 481 - outside expectations for retail activity, suggesting

institutional or other large-value transfers, with a different risk profile. The transactions via VASPs are concentrated (94 percent) in the fiat to virtual assets (VAs) and vice versa conversion rather than crypto exchange activity, which would involve safekeeping and a digital platform. $^{3}$ The number of VASPs grew at the fastest rate in 2025, reaching 188, mostly consisting of VA conversion operators that provide services for the purchase and sale of VA on their own behalf. The NBKR, as a supervisor for banks, has not authorized any of the banks to provide VA services. In addition, 6 issuers of VA are licensed in Kyrgyz Republic, issuing VA valued at more than USD 34 million.

![](images/27398e97fdbf212adc86505723803048ccb2e024db4f3c5e31778e257ea93114.jpg)

4. Rapid growth of the VA and VASP sectors requires an up-to-date understanding of evolving ML/TF risks, with a focus on analysis of the Kyrgyz Republic's financial sector's exposures. The 2023 sectoral VASP assessment provided an analytical foundation using comprehensive methodology, but the sector's fast evolution both in the scale and nature of activity renders some conclusions outdated. For example, the 2023 assessment found that approximately half of customers were non-residents prior to the recent substantial expansion driven by non-resident activity. The authorities should conduct an expedited update of the VA/VASP risk assessment, with particular emphasis on linkages to the banking sector. Specifically, the risk assessment should determine current levels of VASP activity through financial institutions in Kyrgyz Republic, including the on- and off-ramping, volumes of cash transactions, concentration of VASP business in specific institutions, and risk mitigation measures applied by banks. Analysis of the latest VASP transaction data would allow identification of changes in customer base, share of cross-border and non-resident activity, geographic counterparties, and potential red flags to integrate in the national and supervisory risk-based AML/CFT approach.

5. The updated VA/VASP sectoral risk assessment is fundamental to informing risk-based AML/CFT approach on the national level and to supervision of VASPs and banks. The State Financial Supervision Service (SFSS), a supervisor for VASPs, has developed the key components of the toolkit for risk-based supervision, such as data collection from VASPs and risk scoring for individual VASPs based on their business model, client base, transaction profile, and compliance quality, although no VASPs were rated at the highest, "critical" risk level. The risk-based toolkit for VASP supervision should be strengthened, informed by the updated VA/VASP sectoral assessment, with a deeper focus on cross-border activity and geographic ML/TF risk factors. The NBKR should continue integration of VA and VASP-related risk factors into its AML/CFT supervision of banks, including a dedicated focus on VASPs as higher risk clients of banks.

6. Current supervisory resources dedicated to VASPs are insufficient considering the scale of activity and the number of licensed VASPs. The SFSS has 4 staff positions allocated to VASP supervision, but only two staff are currently available. This significant imbalance between the number of VASP supervisory staff and the number of supervised entities needs to be urgently corrected to support implementation of core supervisory functions at a frequency and depth appropriate to the sector's scale and number of supervised entities. Considering the rapid growth of the VASP sector in the last two years, oversight of the VASP sector would be strengthened by a formal supervisory strategy with minimum engagement model feasible given the resource constraints, outlining the minimum supervisory actions for each VASP risk level.

7. Recent changes in the institutional arrangements for VASP regulation and supervision pose challenges to supervisory effectiveness. Recent amendments removed the licensing, policy development, regulatory, and sanctioning functions related to VASPs from the State Financial Supervision Service (SFSS), which now retains only the supervision mandate. These functions have been allocated to a different state agency, creating a fragmented institutional framework and requiring close and effective inter-agency coordination. The separation of supervision from market entry and enforcement powers poses a challenge to effectiveness of VASP supervision. $^{4}$ Licensing provides supervisors with essential information about the ownership structure, business model, risk profile, and internal control systems of VASP applicants, contributing to an effective risk-based supervisory approach from the outset. Supervisory involvement in licensing allows application of their sectoral risk understanding to assess the suitability of applicants, identify red flags, and impose tailored licensing conditions that reflect the specific risk exposures of each entity. Moreover, the ability to deny licenses based on risk considerations, such as opaque ownership structures, inadequate AML/CFT controls, or high-risk business models, is an important preventive tool, without which the supervisors may be forced to oversee entities they would not have approved, limiting their ability to shape the risk profile of the sector proactively. Similarly, ability to sanction identified AML/CFT breaches is critical for a supervisor to credibly address and dissuade high risk activities. In addition, combining the licensing function with a development mandate aimed at promoting the growth of the VASP sector may raise questions regarding the prioritization of objectives and credibility of VASP market entry controls.

8. Recent reforms have strengthened the regulatory framework for VASPs, which should be followed by strengthened measures to ensure fitness and propriety of VASPs' owners and managers. The extension of licensing requirements to beneficial owners of VASPs in 2025 addresses a key technical compliance gap with the FATF Standards. Considering that the current license holders received the license under the earlier regime, review of the fitness and propriety of senior management and beneficial owners of existing VASP should be a supervisory priority. Fitness and propriety tests should use a wider range of sources, including information requests to supervisors abroad, considering global operations and transnational business models of VASPs. The supervisors should also use an appropriate burden of proof in these tests, in which reasonable doubts about the honesty and integrity of senior management and owners should lead to the applicant or license rejection. The withdrawal of 26 licenses over the last three years and no rejected applications on AML/CFT grounds suggest a scope for more robust scrutiny at the licensing stage.

9. Cross-border payment flows through the banking sector in Kyrgyz Republic have also grown strongly and are not fully explained by the main macro-economic fundamentals. The cross-border financial flows have quadrupled since 2022, including with higher risk countries as identified in the NRA, and the outlier patterns identified by the staff. Detection and mitigation of cross-border risks would benefit from national monitoring of payments macro-trends, building on the existing interagency AML/CFT coordination. The accelerated growth, evolving patterns and appearance of new corridors of cross-border payments require an up-to-date understanding of their drivers and risk implications as well as coordinated policy and operational responses.

## C. Trade in Controlled Goods: Monitoring and Enforcement

10. Cross-border trade has grown rapidly and is a key economic growth driver since 2022 with substantial transit and re-export activity. As a trade and logistics hub, Kyrgyz Republic faces risks related to misrepresentation of goods or routing, unauthorized re-export and diversion of controlled goods subject to the national export control regime to unauthorized parties. The rapid increase in re-export activity, partially reflected in balance-of-payments errors and omissions of 17 percent of GDP in the first three quarters of 2025, highlights the materiality of the diversion risks. Controlled goods entering the Kyrgyz Republic may be diverted to third countries in violation of license terms, re-exported without proper authorization, or transshipped through complex routing to obscure ultimate destinations. In addition, smuggling and corruption vulnerabilities exacerbate the risks related to trade in controlled goods.

11. Resources dedicated to monitoring implementation of restrictions for trade in controlled goods have not kept pace with the growth in trade and associated risks. National legislation establishes licensing frameworks for export controls, and the National Control List underwent updates in consultation with international partners, also including goods on the Common High Priority List. The Customs Service has incorporated the risks factors related to the trade in controlled goods into their risk-based customs inspections. However, limited resources constrain the development of the regime to effectively monitor implementation of the export control framework. Specifically, the increase in staffing in the Division of export control and licensing of the Ministry of Economy to 6 persons is not commensurate with the high rates of growth in trade. While the authorities conducted relevant trainings and joined capacity development initiatives, effective export control administration requires dedicated staff and engagement of specialized sectoral skills, particularly for the identification and classification of controlled goods.

12. Effective export control regime requires strengthening post-clearance monitoring targeting highest-risk transactions. The current export control regime focuses on the licensing stage and would benefit from dedicated resources to verify adherence to the license conditions. Exporters are required to provide guarantee letters (end-user certificates) and the authorities,

despite the staffing constraints, have experience of conducting several on-site inspections to confirm end-use in line with the licensing conditions, but the extent of post-clearance controls is disproportionate relative to the licensing activity in recent years. Post-clearance monitoring of implementation of licensing conditions could leverage a risk-based approach, including reviews of import and re-export transactions involving high-risk controlled goods; trading companies with minimal domestic footprint (e.g., no taxes or fees paid); customs declarations not matching declared licensing purposes; entities with non-resident beneficial owners or complex offshore structures. A developed risk-based approach would enable the authorities to maximize the deterrent effect while remaining operationally feasible given the resource constraints and minimizing the trade frictions.

13. Accelerating digitalization and inter-agency information exchange is essential to detect risks related to the trade in controlled goods, considering rapidly increasing trade turnover and insufficient staffing. The 2030 digitalization strategy appropriately includes these reforms, but implementation should be accelerated and prioritized for controlled goods given rapid growth in trade, its importance as a driver of growth as well as associated risks. Specifically, digitalization and automatic exchange of information between the customs databases with the licensing records is instrumental to verify compliance. The detection capabilities of the currently fragmented post-clearance monitoring system, based on manual transaction-by-transaction paper exchange, would benefit from accelerated development of digital systems for cross-referencing the customs data with: (i) tax filings (to identify shell companies with no operations); (ii) FIU reporting (to detect trading companies with suspicious payment patterns); and (iii) beneficial ownership registry (to understand control structures). Recently established Beneficial Ownership Registry holds significant potential to benefit a wide range of state functions and should be promptly operationalized.

## D. Potential Financial Integrity Risk Events and Macroeconomic Transmission Channels

14. A major financial integrity risk event related to elevated cross-border risks could have macro-critical consequences. A high-profile trigger event could bring ML/TF vulnerabilities into international focus and lead to reputational damage, loss of confidence and jurisdictional risk reclassification. $^{5}$ The main transmission channels with the potential to have macro-critical consequences in Kyrgyz Republic are the real sector (through the trade disruptions) and the external sector (through the decrease in capital and other inflows). Specifically, a financial integrity risk event could lead to reversal of the re-export trade resulting from redirection of trade away from Kyrgyz Republic by the foreign counterparts and disruption of trade settlement and trade finance, including delays, enhanced scrutiny, or refusals to process payments or provide financing. On the external side, the risk event could lead to international investors, multilateral and financial institutions de-

risking and restricting exposures to Kyrgyz Republic, which would negatively impact the capital and other inflows as well as banking sector liquidity due to the withdrawal of non-resident and high-risk deposits. $^{6}$ Pressure on already fragile correspondent banking network could result in further frictions in cross-border activity through increased costs and delays in transactions or loss of access by some financial institutions to FX services and cross-border payments. $^{7}$

15. Modeling a financial integrity shock, the economy would grow more slowly, the fiscal deficit would be larger, and the external position would be weaker. For the purposes of the annex, we illustrate the macroeconomic impact of a financial integrity shock, which might be triggered by the FATF grey-listing, media disclosures, or foreign law enforcement or supervisory actions against financial institutions, VASPs or companies registered in Kyrgyz Republic. Specifically, we assume: (i) a quarter reduction in imports meant for re-exports; (ii) a one-third decrease in FDI inflows; and (iii) a 15 percent decrease in private transfers, mostly remittances, into Kyrgyz Republic. These assumptions would result in: (i) lower GDP growth (from 6.1 percent in 2026 in the baseline to 4.9 percent following the AML shock); (ii) a higher budget deficit (from 3.8 percent to 4.6 percent); and (iii) lower reserves (from 3.2 months of prospective imports in 2031 in the baseline to 1.8 months in the AML shock model).

![](images/7fe1c071b7c6e402cb8aa790e967d59abfbcc948c988bedc45b138696c02b823.jpg)

## Status of HIPC and MDRI Assistance

On November 30, 2011, the Executive Board considered the addition of income and indebtedness criteria for end-2010 to the HIPC Initiative framework, which resulted in the removal of the Kyrgyz Republic from the ring-fenced list of eligible countries.

## Safeguards Assessments

An update assessment of the National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic (NBKR) in connection with the emergency financing under the RFI/RCF disbursements, approved by the IMF Executive Board in March and May 2020, was completed on January 13, 2021. The assessment found that the shortcomings in the legal framework of the NBKR as reported in the previous assessment had not been addressed and recommended amendments to the NBKR law.

The Parliament of the Kyrgyz Republic amended the NBKR law in 2022, but did not fully address the 2020 Safeguards Assessment recommendations. The amended law envisages a proper recapitalization and profit distribution mechanisms, which is an important accomplishment, but does not introduce a majority of non-executive members of the NBKR Board and the Audit Committee, or provisions to wind down non-core central bank operations. In addition, the laws passed by Parliament in each of the last four years suspended the provisions of the NBKR law on profit distribution to allow for transfers of NBKR's profit to the budget even though NBKR capital fell below the statutory floor.

## Exchange Rate Arrangements

The de jure exchange rate arrangement is a floating arrangement. The NBKR participates and intervenes in the interbank foreign exchange market to limit exchange rate volatility as necessary. During 2025, the flexibility of the exchange rate against the U.S. dollar was limited. The de facto classification of the exchange rate arrangement is “crawl-like” and has remained unchanged since October 21, 2022.

The NBKR publishes daily the exchange rate of the som in terms of the U.S. dollar, which is determined in the interbank foreign exchange market. The official exchange rate of the som against the dollar is calculated as the daily weighted average of the exchange rates used in the purchase and sale transactions of dollars conducted in the foreign exchange market through the Automated Trading System (ATS) of the NBKR for the reporting period from 3:00 pm of the previous trading day to 3:00 pm of the current trading day. The official exchange rate becomes effective from the next working day. The government uses the official exchange rate for budget and tax accounting purposes as well as for all payments between the government and enterprises and other legal entities.

The Kyrgyz Republic accepted the obligations under Article VIII, Section 2, 3, and 4 of the IMF's Articles of Agreement and maintains exchange system free of multiple currency practices and restrictions on the making of payments and transfers for current international transactions, except for the exchange restrictions for security reasons related to restrictions of financial transactions and the freeze of accounts of certain individuals or organizations associated with terrorism pursuant to (i) relevant U.N. Security Council resolutions; and (ii) the list of current terrorist organizations designated by the U.S. Secretary of State. The authorities have notified these measures to the Fund in May 2007 pursuant to Executive Board decision No. 144-(52/51).

## Article IV Consultations

The Kyrgyz Republic is on a 12-month consultation cycle. The 2025 Article IV consultation discussions were held in March 2025 and were completed by the Executive Board in May 2025.

## FSAP Participation and ROSC Assessment

An FSAP update mission in July 2013 reviewed progress since the 2007 assessment, and the Board discussed the Financial System Stability Assessment (FSSA) along with the fifth ECF review in December 2013. The FSSA was not published. A fiscal ROSC mission was held in March 2001 and the ROSC Fiscal Transparency Module was published on March 13, 2002. A data ROSC mission was held in November 2002 and the ROSC Data Module was published in November 2003. A fiscal ROSC reassessment was held in September 2007.

## Resident Representative

The twelfth resident representative of the Fund in the Kyrgyz Republic, Mr. Farid Talishli, took his post in Bishkek in July 2023.

# KYRGYZ REPUBLIC

May 14, 2026

## STAFF REPORT FOR THE 2026 ARTICLE IV CONSULTATION—DEBT SUSTAINABILITY ANALYSIS

Approved By
Subir Lall and Cemile
Sancak (IMF) and Abebe
Adugna and Manuela
Francisco (IDA)

Prepared by staffs of the International Monetary Fund and the International Development Association

<table><tr><td>Risk of external debt distress</td><td>Moderate</td></tr><tr><td>Overall risk of debt distress</td><td>Moderate</td></tr><tr><td>Granularity in the risk rating</td><td>Some space to absorb shocks</td></tr><tr><td>Application of judgment</td><td>No</td></tr></table>

Staff assess the Kyrgyz Republic's public debt as sustainable with moderate risks of external and overall debt distress. $^{1}$ There is a breach of the relevant thresholds for three out of four external debt ratios under the most extreme standard stress test (a standard shock to exports in all four cases). In addition, there is a breach of the benchmark for the PV of total public debt to GDP ratio under the most extreme standard shock (growth). However, under the baseline, the PV of public debt increases from 34.3 percent of GDP in 2025 to 38.1 percent by 2036, remaining below its benchmark and therefore consistent with a moderate risk of total debt distress. Similarly, none of the external debt ratios breach their thresholds under the baseline, leading to a moderate risk of external debt distress. Because the external debt burden indicators trend downward and the breaches are limited, staff assess external and total public debt to be sustainable. Creating fiscal space and containing debt vulnerabilities will require improving tax collection, sustaining efforts to reduce the wage bill and energy subsidies, strengthening debt management, seeking concessional financing and improving public investment management.

## PUBLIC DEBT COVERAGE

1. Public and Publicly Guaranteed (PPG) debt covers state government debt (both central and local government), state guarantees, and the debt of the central bank to the IMF (Text Table 1). All public sector debt is currently issued by the central government. Going forward, planned Eurobond issuances by two state-owned banks would introduce publicly guaranteed debt as a new component of public sector liabilities. The part of the 2021 SDR allocation that has been drawn down is counted toward public debt. Local governments have no external debt and insignificant domestic debt. The social security fund has no debt. State-owned enterprises (SOEs) mostly borrow from the government and, by law, do not incur external debt. SOEs short-term domestic borrowing from the banking sector is limited and do not have long-term domestic debt. In addition, the government has no outstanding external debt guarantees. $^{2}$ As of October 2025, there is no significant PPG debt for new companies created as Public-Private Partnerships. As in the previous DSA, given the large stock of liabilities associated with the energy sector (equivalent to around 15 percent of GDP in 2022 $^{3}$ ), and the 2024 conversion into depreciated equity of energy SOE debt, a contingent liability shock of 13 percent of GDP is applied, of which 8 percent of GDP are potential expenditures not included in the baseline, reflecting an operational risk stemming from the structural cash shortfalls of loss-making energy sector SOEs $^{4}$ despite recent tariff increases; and 5 percent of GDP is the default value representing the average cost to the government during a financial crisis (Text Table 2).

## BACKGROUND

## 2. Public debt increased to 39.4 percent of GDP in 2025 from 36.2 percent of GDP in 2024

(Text Figure 1). This increase was driven by a rise in domestic debt. External debt declined from 24.9 percent of GDP to 23.6 percent of GDP, mainly reflecting strong nominal GDP growth and a broadly stable exchange rate. In contrast, domestic debt increased from 11.3 percent of GDP to 15.8 percent of GDP, largely driven by new lending to Government from Eldik Bank. Domestic public debt is held predominantly by commercial banks and the social security fund.

3. External debt continues to be predominantly denominated in US dollars and SDRs. These two currencies account for around 85 percent of nominal external debt at end-2025 (Text Figure 2). The euro accounts for less than 5 percent.

![](images/bae8059fd63ead159e47d65e29830f7e8f3f989abb42dc2ccb6432ba44e772da.jpg)  
Text Figure 1. Public Debt, 2015-2025 (Percent of GDP)

![](images/b7e6aa89b897159267e4d58fb1a1e50e2ed344ef892dbcad130cfd7e004c111a.jpg)  
Source: Country Authorities

![](images/e84cf786b6742d24ff3f2d78133a83ffd3ab5e063adcb5b3b6b5d774e915984a.jpg)

4. China remains the largest creditor at end 2025. $^{5}$ Official external debt owed to China accounts for 28.1 percent of total external debt, down from 36.5 percent at end 2024. The International Development Association (IDA–World Bank) accounts for over 17 percent of total external debt, followed by the Asian Development Bank (ADB) at over 16 percent (Text Figure 3). The IMF is owed 3.8 percent of the total, down from over 7 percent in 2024. As in the last DSA, the large creditors in the "Other" category include Japan, the Islamic Development Bank, other bilateral lenders, and the Eurasian Development Bank.

5. According to the Adaptation and Resilience assessment conducted for the Country Climate and Development Report (CCDR), the Kyrgyz Republic is vulnerable to climate shocks due to its mountainous terrain, significant agricultural production, and hydropower investment commitments. $^{6}$ On September 1, 2025, the authorities officially adopted the National Green Economy Development Program for 2025-29, which aims to strengthen the focus on green investments, enhance energy efficiency, and modernize the agricultural sector. The baseline reflects the current policy mix, including the implementation of the Kyrgyz Republic's climate strategy, of which the Kambarata hydro power plant is an important component.

## UNDERLYING ASSUMPTIONS

6. The macroeconomic outlook (Text Table 3) illustrates the economy's strong recent performance and robust prospects.

\- Growth and inflation. Growth remained very strong in 2025 at 11.1 percent, extending the momentum of the previous three years, mainly driven by the continued expansion in trade and the robust activity in transport, communications, and construction. $^{7}$ Growth is projected to

moderate to 6.1 percent in 2026 as reexport trade-related activities plateau; it will remain modestly above potential through 2028, supported by the start of the large hydropower project and high construction activity, and converge to the potential in the medium and long term as the spillovers from Russia's invasion of Ukraine subside. Compared to the previous DSA, the stronger growth outlook reflects an upward revision to a higher potential output growth, which is now estimated at around 5.3 percent. $^{8}$ Inflation is projected to remain elevated in the near term—at 12.5 percent in 2026 and 10.5 percent in 2027—before gradually moderating to 6.5 percent by 2031.

\- Fiscal policy. The overall budget balance recorded surpluses for three consecutive years, at about 1.6, 2.0 and 2.6 percent of GDP in 2023, 2024 and 2025, respectively. During 2025, tax collection remained strong, reflecting continued revenue overperformance and a compositional shift away from VAT on imports toward corporate income tax and taxes on international trade, partly driven by the introduction of the simplified taxation regime as an alternative to the VAT. Non-tax revenues remained a key factor supporting the fiscal position, mainly driven by proceeds from the liquidation of seized assets and profit transfers from the NBKR. On the expenditure side, pressures reflected wage increases and higher-than-budgeted transfers to the Stabilization Fund, $^{9}$ while other spending categories remained broadly in line with budget allocations. Capitalization of SOEs—particularly in the banking and energy sectors—led to a significant increase in the net acquisition of financial assets and, in turn, higher financing needs. The fiscal balance is projected to shift into a deficit of 3.8 percent of GDP in 2026, following strong outcomes over the past three years. This deterioration reflects a large salary increase for education and health workers, and increased spending on major infrastructure projects outside the Public Investment Program (PIP), notably the Kambarata-1 hydropower plant and the China–Kyrgyzstan–Uzbekistan railway, alongside continued on-lending to loss-making energy SOEs, which authorities classify below the line (Box 1). In addition, planned capital injections into other SOEs and Eldik Bank are expected to add about 2.3 percent of GDP to financing needs. Even though gold accounts for around 2.5 percent of revenue, the deficit is further exacerbated by lower non-tax revenues and a modest decline in trade-related tax revenues. Over the medium term, claims on resources (including the net acquisition of financial assets) are projected to decline gradually as state-owned banks are expected to borrow directly from the markets, thus helping to anchor net financing needs below 3 and $\frac{1}{2}$ percent of GDP, despite rising interest payments.

\- External sector. The current account deficit is estimated to have reached 24.4 percent of GDP in 2025, which was partly offset by BoP errors and omissions of 17.2 percent of GDP. The latter reflect unrecorded re-exports to the Eurasian Customs Union (EACU), mostly from China to Russia, as intra-EACU trade is not subject to mandatory reporting and customs monitoring. Accounting for re-exports, the current account deficit would reach 7.5 percent of GDP in 2025 and 7.3 percent of GDP in 2026, with exports of locally-produced gold accounting for over 40 percent of all exports. It will decrease further to 5.3 percent by 2031 and remain around 5

percent by 2046. Gross international reserves are projected to decline from 6.0 months of imports to 3.2 months in 2031, and hover around 3.0 months by 2046.

## Box 1. Major Infrastructure Projects

\- The Kambarata-1 HPP is an energy project. The total cost of the project is estimated to be US\$4.5 billion, incurred between 2026-36. The Kambarata-1 HPP project will be financed and implemented jointly by the Kyrgyz Republic, Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan, based on a cooperation agreement between the governments of these three countries. The Kyrgyz Republic will contribute 34 percent and Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan 33 percent each. The projected fiscal balance in this DSA accounts for only Kyrgyz Republic's share in expected project costs.

\- The China-Kyrgyzstan-Uzbekistan (CKU) railway project is a significant infrastructure initiative aimed at enhancing connectivity and trade between China, Kyrgyzstan, and Uzbekistan. The cost of the project, which is designed to operate commercially and profitably, is estimated at US\$4.7 billion and expected to be built during 2026-32. The parties agreed to implement the project according to the TOT (construction - operation - transfer) model by creating a joint project company with the distribution of shares in the following proportions: 51% China Railway, 24.5% Kyrgyz side (SE NC Kyrgyz Temir Zholu), and 24.5% Uzbek side (Uzbekistan Railways). Financing of the project is expected to be carried out in the following proportions: 50% of financing will be equity, 50% will be borrowed funds (loans from Chinese financial institutions). It should be noted that the borrower will be the Joint Project Company (JSC). The projected fiscal balance in this DSA accounts for only Kyrgyz Republic's share in expected project costs.

• Financing assumptions. New external borrowing is assumed to reflect a mix of concessional and non-concessional financing. IDA loan disbursements are expected at US\$133.4 million in 2026 and to increase gradually to around US\$180 million in 2030 including disbursement for the construction of the Kambarata-1 HPP. IDA financing remains concessional, despite the hardened terms associated with allocations from the latest replenishment. The ADB is projected to provide US\$50 million as budget support and US\$55 million for projects in 2026, plus another US\$50 million and US\$70 million respectively in 2027. The ADB is projected to lend about US\$590 million between 2028-31 – the amounts include budgetary support, PIP external loans and support for the construction of the Kambarata-1 HPP.

\- A Eurobond issuance of US700 million took place in 2025. $^{10}$ Going forward, one large state-owned bank is expected to issue US500 million in 2026, followed by another state-owned bank issuing US300 million in 2027. In line with the authorities' plan to raise US1.7 billion in total, a further issuance of US200 million is assumed in 2028. In addition, a syndicated loan of US333 million on commercial terms (estimated at around 12 percent), to be mobilized in 2026 with IDA/MIGA guarantee support, is included in the baseline. Separately, about US1.2 billion in financing from China is projected over the next four years through various instruments to

support the railway project. Finally, the Government is projected to secure on average an additional US\$50 million per year in external loans over the medium term. These borrowings on less-than-concessional terms are expected to reduce the average concessionality of external debt over time. As external financing gradually declines, domestic debt is projected to replace external debt, including the Eurobond slated for amortization in 2030; the share of net domestic borrowing in total financing (excluding external amortization) is projected to increase from around 57 percent in 2031—when external project financing remains elevated—to about 65 percent by 2046. Domestic borrowing rates are assumed at 9–16 percent for maturities of 1 to 7 years in 2026, declining gradually to 9–13 percent by 2031 as domestic financial markets deepen.

## 7. Baseline projections are realistic:

\- Drivers of debt dynamics. The forecast error of the change in the ratio of public debt-to-GDP over the past five years has been significant (Figure 3). Total public debt in 2031 is expected to increase modestly to 41.3 percent of GDP in nominal terms, but only to 37.8 percent of GDP in PV terms – thanks to mostly concessional external borrowing. The gradual decline in the total public debt-to-GDP ratio beyond 2031 is explained by a narrowing of the general government deficit.

\- Realism of planned fiscal adjustment (Figure 4). The projected 3-year adjustment shows a deterioration of the primary balance due to the deterioration in tax revenue and a normalization of nontax revenue.

\- Consistency between fiscal adjustment and growth (Figure 4). The growth projection for 2026 is below the growth paths suggested by different fiscal multipliers, as recent growth performance is not expected to be sustained. The projected fiscal expansion for 2026 reflects the expected decline in trade-related tax revenue, the reversal to the norm of nontax revenue, and new infrastructure spending.

\- Consistency between public investment and growth (Figure 4). Medium- and long-term growth is projected at close to its potential rate of around 5.3 percent, which is consistent with the path for public investment.

## COUNTRY CLASSIFICATION AND STRESS TESTS

8. The Kyrgyz Republic's debt-carrying capacity has become strong for the first time (Text Table 4). The country's Composite Indicator (CI) index $^{11}$ is 3.15 for its most recent vintage, above the 3.05 threshold for strong debt-carrying capacity. The CI score has also remained above 3.05 in the previous two vintages, supporting a strong debt-carrying capacity assessment. $^{12}$ The current CI score is calculated based on the IMF World Economic Outlook (WEO) vintage of October 2025 and the World Bank's 2024 CPIA vintage. Strong debt-carrying capacity classification translates into the following external debt burden thresholds, which are higher than for the previous DSA: 240 percent of the present value (PV) of PPG external debt-to-exports ratio, 55 percent of the PV of PPG external debt-to-GDP, 21 percent of the PPG external debt service-to-exports, and 23 percent of the PPG external debt service-to-revenue. The total public debt benchmark is 70 percent of the PV of total public debt-to-GDP ratio.

Sources: Country authorities; and IMF and World Bank staff.

## EXTERNAL DSA

## 9. All four external debt ratios remain below their respective thresholds under the

5. All four external debt ratios remain below their respective thresholds under the baseline scenario (Figure 1, and Tables 1 and 3). External Public and Publicly Guaranteed (PPG) debt was 23.6 percent of GDP at end-2025 and private external debt was estimated at 20.9 percent of GDP. The latter is expected to decline to around 19.9 percent of GDP starting in 2026 as net external private borrowing is projected to grow less than nominal GDP. The PV of PPG external debt slightly decreased to 18.5 percent of GDP in 2025 (from 21.8 percent in the previous DSA, based on a lower nominal GDP). It is projected to decrease to 15.2 percent of GDP by end-2036 (well below the 55 percent threshold) on the back of decreasing external borrowing over the next ten years, and the amortization of Eurobonds between 2030 and 2033 . (Text Figure 4). The high residual up to 2025 is attributable to the high errors and omissions in the BOP, which are not projected onward (Table 1).

10. Three out of four external debt indicators breach their respective thresholds under the most extreme standard stress test (exports), with breaches occurring in the medium term. In particular, the PV of external debt-to-GDP ratio remains above its threshold during 2028–30 before declining. The debt service-to-exports ratio breaches its threshold in 2030–31 and again in 2033, while the debt service-to-revenue ratio breaches in 2030. These breaches reflect higher external

borrowing to finance infrastructure projects, less concessional borrowing terms associated with Eurobond issuances, and the historically high volatility of exports. All indicators under the most extreme standard stress tests begin to improve by 2034 and fall back below their respective thresholds by the end of the projection period (2036).

## PUBLIC DSA

11. The PV of debt-to-GDP ratio, used to assess the risk of total public debt distress, remains contained and below the benchmark despite a moderate increase under the baseline scenario (Figure 2 and Tables 2 and 4). Public debt increased from 36.2 percent of GDP in 2024 to 39.4 percent in 2025, reflecting the Eurobond issuance and high domestic financing needs despite strong nominal GDP growth. It increases further to 42.3 percent in 2026 due to continued high financing needs and rising interest payments (Text Table 4). Thereafter, the ratio stabilizes at around 41 percent of GDP over the medium term and decreases gradually to 39 percent by 2046. At the same time, the debt service-to-revenue ratio (including grants) rises from 12.3 percent in 2025 to 23.8 percent in 2031 and 2036, and around 29 percent by 2046, reflecting elevated domestic interest rates and higher external interest and amortization payments associated with non-concessional borrowing.

12. As in the previous DSA, total public debt is vulnerable to a growth shock, which is the most extreme stress test. Under this shock, the PV of debt-to-GDP ratio breaches the benchmark of 70 percent by 2031 and continues a persistent upward trajectory thereafter. This shock also puts two other indicators on an upward path: the PV of debt-to-revenue ratio would reach over 300 percent by 2036, and the debt service-to-revenue ratio would reach over 53 percent also in 2036. Although no explicit benchmark exists for these two ratios, the projections point to potential debt vulnerabilities in the long run.

13. The projection of domestic public debt indicator for liquidity remains below the median for LIC DSF user countries, but the one for solvency increases above it (Figure 6). Over the medium-term (2026-32), the domestic debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to increase and remain above its historical range and the median of domestic debt-to-GDP ratios across countries using the

LIC DSF (17.1 percent), starting in 2031. The domestic public debt service-to-revenue ratio is projected to increase monotonously as of 2030, but will remain below the median of this ratio for countries using LIC DSF of (21.7 percent) over the entire forecast period.

14. Domestic debt management should be strengthened. The priority measures include revising the Debt Management Strategy in line with the standards of the MTDS framework, including its planned implementation through an Annual Borrowing Plan and Issuance Calendar; improving collaboration between the NBKR and the Ministry of Finance to strengthen liquidity management and forecasting; setting medium term targets for average Term-to-Maturity to design a better mix of domestic securities; defining debt management objectives within the Budget Code and the medium-term horizon of the cost and risk parameters; developing and publishing short-term yield curve based on NBKR notes and T-Bill auctions; lifting interest rate caps and yield change limits, and allowing full market price discovery in auctions; implementing a benchmark bond policy; issuing 3-month and 6-month T-Bills at market rate; and strengthening technical and institutional capacity of Public Debt Management Department. In addition, the authorities should exercise caution in managing their exposure to external borrowing, including planned Eurobond issuance by state-owned banks, which would add to publicly guaranteed debt. The authorities' reform agenda in debt management has been supported by the IDA's Sustainable Development Finance Policy. The most recent reforms included the approved methodological guidelines on preparation of i) an annual public debt report and ii) an annual SOEs aggregate report including SOEs' financial performance assessment.

## A. Risk Rating and Vulnerabilities

15. The mechanical risk rating suggested by the baseline and the stress tests supports staffs' assessment of external debt as being at moderate risk of debt distress. The breach of a threshold by three out of four external debt ratios under a standard stress test on exports leads to a "moderate" risk rating of external debt distress. This assessment is further supported by the heightened uncertainty stemming from the amplitude and rapidity of the decrease in exports mirroring equally abrupt historical movements, and the rise in downside global and regional risks that are not well captured under the standard tests. These risks include possible reversals of the rapid expansion of the re-export trade, higher global interest rates, and the limited diversification of exports. These factors all argue for a "moderate" rating for the risk of external debt distress. Moreover, staff assess external debt as sustainable, because there are no breaches of relevant thresholds under the baseline for any external debt indicators, and they are all projected on a downward trajectory after 2030, driven by the decline in PPG external debt after the substantial repayment of externally issued securities by 2030.

16. The risk of overall public debt distress remains “moderate.” This reflects the “moderate” rating for the risk of external debt distress, plus the breach by the PV of debt-to-GDP ratio of its benchmark of 70 percent before 2036 under a standard stress test on growth. The upward trajectory of this ratio is driven by growing domestic borrowing as a residual source of financing at relatively high interest rates in the absence of more concessional finance. Over time, interest payments become a significant expenditure item and drive financing needs, and refinancing

challenge triggered by the bullet repayment of the Eurobond appear clearly in the "kink" in 2030 of the debt service/revenue ratio. The moderate risk rating is further justified by the debt management weaknesses. However, total public debt is still assessed as sustainable since the ratio of PV of public debt-to-GDP under the baseline remains well below 70 percent of GDP by 2036, even though it slightly increases. This is consistent with the finding of "some fiscal space" evidenced by the granularity tool in Figure 5.

17. Risks to the ratings assessment are significant. A tightening of Western sanctions on Russia would worsen prospects for the Russian economy, leading to depreciation of the ruble and the som, and lower remittances, which in turn would weaken the current account deficit and raise external debt service. Conversely, higher oil prices amid ongoing conflict in the Middle East, together with a potential easing of sanctions on Russia, could improve the outlook for the Russian economy, with a mix of positive spillovers through stronger remittances and exchange rate support and adverse effects such as the higher energy import bill. However, an easing of sanctions could also unwind some trade flows and reduce growth. These risks could be further compounded by higher commodity prices, a prolonged conflict in the Middle East, extreme weather events, and cyber threats.

18. Additional fiscal space will be necessary to mitigate future shocks. The DSA implies a debt stabilizing primary balance of less than one percent of GDP in the medium term (Table 2). The breach of the benchmark for the PV of Debt-to-GDP ratio under a standard stress test on growth by 2031 indicates that the Kyrgyz Republic needs fiscal buffers to address unexpected shocks. This can be achieved by improving revenue mobilization, including by streamlining tax exemptions and strengthening tax administration, reducing the wage bill and energy subsidies, improved targeting of social benefits, improving public investment management, seeking more concessional financing, and strengthening debt management. The projected increase in domestic financing creates additional risks to the economy as it could crowd out lending to the private sector. A new drive to reduce the state footprint in the economy should result in faster growth, lower contingent liabilities, and stronger donor engagement.

## AUTHORITIES' VIEWS

19. The authorities broadly agreed with the views of Bank and Fund staff. They noted the additional space afforded by higher long-term growth, which helps dampen debt accumulation and keeps it well below their nominal debt ceiling of 70 percent of GDP. They recognized the risks associated with new borrowing on commercial terms and emphasized the importance of carefully sequencing external debt repayments to avoid liquidity pressures on the treasury and risks to external reserves. They reiterated their commitment to prudent fiscal and debt policies, supported by continued improvements in tax administration. They noted that Eurobond issuances by state-owned banks do not involve explicit guarantees.

1/ Includes both public and private sector external debt.  
2/ Derived as $(r - g - p(1 + g) + \varepsilon\alpha(1 + r)/(1 + g + p + g\rho))$ times previous period debt ratio, with r = nominal interest rate; g = real GDP growth rate, p = growth rate of GDP deflator in U.S. dollar terms, $\varepsilon=$ nominal appreciation of the local currency, and $\alpha=$ share of local currency-denominated external debt in total external debt.  
4/ Current-year interest payments divided by previous period debt stock.  
5/ Defined as grants, concessional loans, and debt relief.  
6/ Grant-equivalent financing includes grants provided directly to the government and through new borrowing (difference between the face value and the PV of new debt).  
7/ Assumes that PV of private sector debt is equivalent to its face value.

Sources: Country authorities; and staff estimates and projections.  
1/ Coverage of debt: The central, state, and local governments, central bank, government-guaranteed debt. Definition of external debt is Residency-based.  
2/ The underlying PV of external debt-to-GDP ratio under the public DSA differs from the external DSA with the size of differences depending on exchange rates projections.  
3/ Debt service is defined as the sum of interest and amortization of medium and long-term, and short-term debt.  
4/ Gross financing need is defined as the primary deficit plus debt service plus the stock of short-term debt at the end of the last period and other debt creating/reducing flows.  
5/ Defined as a primary deficit minus a change in the public debt-to-GDP ratio ((-): a primary surplus), which would stabilize the debt ratio only in the year in question.  
6/ Historical averages are generally derived over the past 10 years, subject to data availability, whereas projections averages are over the first year of projection and the next 10 years.

1/ The most extreme stress test is the test that yields the highest ratio in or before 2036. The stress test with a one-off breach is also presented (if any), while the one-off breach is deemed away for mechanical signals. When a stress test with a one-off breach happens to be the most exterme shock even after disregarding the one-off breach, only that stress test (with a one-off breach) would be presented.

# Statement by Mr. Loszewski and Ms. Kadyrberdieva on Kyrgyz Republic June 3, 2026

On behalf of our Kyrgyz authorities, we thank Mr. Gershenson and his team for the constructive dialogue as well as the comprehensive and valuable set of reports.

## Recent economic developments

The Kyrgyz Republic continues to record the highest economic growth rate in Central Asia for the third consecutive year, with real GDP increasing by 11.1 percent in 2025. Growth was supported mainly by private consumption and public investments, which led to sectoral growth in construction, industry, and services. This strong economic momentum continued into the current year, with real GDP expanding by 12.4 percent year-on-year over January-April 2026, supporting employment and wages as the country transitions from its low-income status. Job growth accelerated significantly in 2024, with the number of newly created jobs increasing by 141 percent compared to 2023. This momentum was sustained in 2025, although at a more moderate pace, with an annual increase of 4 percent. In addition, labor market formalization has improved. Unemployment steadily declined from 5.3 percent in 2021 to 3.7 percent in 2024. As a result, average household wages increased by about 36 percent between 2022 and 2024, contributing to nearly three-quarters of the decline in poverty rates from 29.8 percent in 2023 to 25.7 percent in 2024.

At the same time, inflationary pressures—particularly in food, fuel, and services—have persisted. Inflation rose from 6.3 percent in 2024 to 9.4 percent in 2025. As of May 15, annual inflation reached 10.9 percent, exceeding the National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic’s (NBKR) target range of 5-7 percent. The underlying drivers of inflation are increasingly non-monetary in nature, reflecting a combination of external factors and entrenched domestic conditions. These include higher electricity and utility tariffs, rising prices in the hospitality sector associated with ongoing fiscalization reforms in tax administration, and strong domestic demand. Monetary drivers of inflation—stemming from the expansion of the money supply through both fiscal and monetary channels—have been actively sterilized by monetary policy instruments and mitigated by the maintenance of stability in the domestic foreign exchange market.

The country's fiscal position strengthened considerably, with the government recording surpluses in the last three years since 2023. Tax revenues rose sharply, driven by higher corporate income tax collections and increased taxes on international trade, creating fiscal space for greater spending on public wages, interest payments, and capital investment. Combined with strong GDP growth, this contributed to a decline in public debt. The country has established a presence in international capital markets through its inaugural Eurobond issuance. The state-owned Eldik Bank successfully issued Eurobonds on international capital markets in April 2026 with the proceeds directed toward lending to key economic sectors, financing environmental, social, and governance projects, and developing the green economy. The financial sector is experiencing robust expansion, driven in part by the rapid growth of virtual assets and credit to the economy. While commercial banks remain largely profitable, well-capitalized, and resilient, these dynamics warrant close monitoring as they may pose risks to financial stability.

The external environment has remained broadly favorable. At the regional level, cooperation among Central Asian countries has strengthened, facilitating greater cross-border trade and investment. Long-standing border disputes with Tajikistan and Uzbekistan have been resolved, alleviating tensions that had persisted for decades. International reserves have increased, while remittances continue to serve as an important buffer supporting household welfare.

## Outlook and risks

The Kyrgyz government has reinforced administrative continuity and reform implementation, advancing new governance initiatives. Adopted in June 2025, the National Development Program through 2030 has continued to guide reform implementation, with a focus on structural transformation, industrialization, economic diversification beyond the extractive sector, and the development of green energy and logistics.

Large-scale infrastructure projects continue to be central to the authorities' development strategy. The Kambarata-1 Hydropower Project, a flagship regional initiative jointly pursued with Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, has moved from initial preparation toward active implementation, with continued technical, environmental, and financing work supported by the World Bank Group and other development partners and expectations that the project will materially strengthen energy security and regional cooperation once completed. In parallel, construction of the China-Kyrgyzstan-Uzbekistan railway is also progressing, marking a step toward transforming Kyrgyzstan into a key regional transit hub and reducing transport bottlenecks for trade with China and broader Eurasian markets. The global mountain agenda has also been elevated as a national priority, with a focus on promoting sustainable mountain development, preserving mountain ecosystems, and strengthening the resilience and livelihoods of mountain communities.

The authorities acknowledge the risks posed by geopolitical tensions and global economic uncertainty, including potential impacts from higher energy prices. However, they believe that the fundamental economic trajectory remains sound, supported by ongoing reforms, major investment projects, and prudent policy management.

## Fiscal policy

The authorities entered 2026 with a prudent fiscal stance, anchored in a state budget adopted with a surplus. This reflects strong revenue mobilization and a firm commitment to medium-term fiscal sustainability, including prioritizing debt repayment and financing capital and social spending within available resources. The authorities remain committed to financing critical investments in infrastructure and human capital while strengthening revenue administration.

Reducing fiscal risks remains a key priority. Fiscal policy continues to be guided by the objective of fostering an enabling environment for private sector-led growth, job creation, and enhanced investment attractiveness. In this regard, the authorities are advancing efforts to broaden the tax base, reduce the shadow and informal economy, improve transparency, and simplify taxation for businesses. Ongoing measures include expanding the taxpayer base—particularly through the formalization of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs)—and further digitalizing tax and customs administration to enhance efficiency and revenue collection. Thus, the authorities consider any increase in the progressivity of the personal income tax to be premature and do not envisage pursuing such changes over the medium term.

The authorities remain committed to strengthening public financial and project management. Efforts are also underway to enhance the governance and efficiency of state-owned enterprises. The authorities recognize the importance of carefully assessing the public pension system to safeguard long-term fiscal sustainability. The authorities view the Stabilization Fund (SF) as an extra-budgetary fund that plays an important role in safeguarding fiscal resilience and supporting sustainable socio-economic development. The SF serves both savings and stabilization functions, including: (i) ensuring continuity of socio-economic development during global downturns; (ii) mitigating the impact of adverse external shocks on the domestic economy and public finances; and (iii) providing resources for deficit financing to address unforeseen expenditures and maintain budget balance in the event of revenue shortfalls. Together with measures to further improve expenditure efficiency, these reforms are aimed at reducing fiscal vulnerabilities and limiting reliance on external borrowing.

Prudent debt management remains central to the authorities' policy framework. While flagship infrastructure projects continue to be financed primarily through concessional lending from international financial institutions, the authorities are mindful of the costs and risks associated with external borrowing on commercial terms and recognize the importance of careful planning of external debt maturities to avoid pressure on the treasury's liquidity position and risks to international reserves. The authorities emphasize that external financing is expected to be directed predominantly toward high-return infrastructure investments.

## Monetary policy and financial sector

Monetary conditions were tightened amid intensifying external and domestic inflationary factors. Monetary policy remains focused on containing inflation and safeguarding financial stability, with the NBKR maintaining a price-stability-oriented framework aimed at achieving inflation targets within the range of 5-7 percent in the medium term.

The inflation outlook in the Kyrgyz Republic remains sensitive to external conditions. The primary external risks are linked to ongoing geopolitical tensions, price volatility in global food markets, and potential disruptions in global supply chains. Domestic inflation factors are more predictable in nature and are primarily driven by sustained domestic demand and the implementation of tariff policies. Collectively, these conditions shape the prospective trajectory of inflation, with risks mostly stemming from external factors.

Against this backdrop, the NBKR continues to closely monitor and assess inflation drivers. Consequently, the NBKR's key policy rate has been maintained at 12 percent while establishing a symmetric and narrower interest rate corridor. On May 26, the overnight deposit rate—used to remunerate banks' excess liquidity—was further increased from 6 to 10 percent. Effective June 2025, the NBKR transitioned to issuing longer-term notes, specifically with maturities of three and six months. Sustaining tight monetary conditions will create a solid foundation for bringing inflation down to the 5-7 percent range over the medium term.

In the financial sector, the NBKR remains well-positioned to address and closely monitor elevated levels of non-performing loans and rapid credit expansion. The authorities recognize the challenges associated with crypto assets and are actively addressing emerging risks stemming from the rapidly evolving virtual assets sector. Efforts are underway to establish a robust regulatory response, supported by clearly defined institutional mandates. In parallel, the authorities are committed to strengthening the effectiveness of AML/CFT frameworks, while enhancing the supervision and governance of Virtual Asset Service Providers.

In response to Western sanctions affecting Kyrgyzstan's financial sector, the authorities have adopted a two-pronged strategy. This approach combines targeted domestic enforcement measures—aimed at preventing exposure to secondary sanctions—with active diplomatic

engagement to address and mitigate external constraints. Key measures undertaken include conducting audits of the banking sector, strengthening oversight of virtual asset activities, suspending the operations of firms exposed to sanctions risks, and maintaining ongoing diplomatic dialogue with international partners.

## Structural reforms

The economic policy agenda remains focused on preserving macroeconomic stability and strengthening resilience, while recognizing the need for further reforms to reduce informality, enhance competition, and foster more private sector-driven and inclusive growth. The authorities are committed to advancing governance reforms, including the effective implementation of the 2025 Anti-Corruption Strategy.

Reducing informality remains critical to achieving inclusive growth. SMEs account for approximately 50 percent of GDP; however, they continue to face regulatory constraints, and relatively few are able to scale. In response, reform efforts are now underway to implement an improved Investment Law with stronger investor protections, improved arbitration mechanisms, and streamlined licensing and permitting processes through digitalization and the reduction of administrative burdens. Continued efforts to improve skills matching, labor mobility, and access to finance for SMEs will be essential to facilitating their transition to the formal sector.

The authorities also recognize the transformative potential of artificial intelligence and are committed to harnessing its applications to improve public service delivery and support broader socio-economic development. In this context, they plan to further strengthen the regulatory framework and continue investing in digital infrastructure.
